## AI Research

### [Resolution has a new Agent Foundations team](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qTNm8qzqhhpno58fZ/resolution-has-a-new-agent-foundations-team)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>The team will include me (Jeremy Gillen), Abram Demski, Sam Eisenstat, Scott Garrabrant and Kaarel Hänni. We'll soon recruit additional experienced researchers and later we plan to hire interns and junior researchers.</span></p><p><span>The team will continue agent foundations research in the spirit of the MIRI Agent Foundations team. This means we’ll be trying to create new theory for understanding minds.</span></p><p><span>Fundamental changes in how we understand minds are necessary before we can build superintelligent systems that enhance human agency rather than cause the extinction of all life on earth. Most fields of engineering are able to reason precisely about unseen scenarios and make design decisions based on this reasoning. The field of AI lacks this basic capability. Agent Foundations can be seen as trying to make this possible by giving us the theoretical grounding to ask different and more precise questions about how ASI will behave after extensive learning, self-modification and interaction with other agents. The questions raised in past agent foundations research point toward much of what we need to know here.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4ftyw94dmhu"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4ftyw94dmhu">[1]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>Alongside the x-risk motivation, I think it’s valuable to motivate research with curiosity. The questions that come up in Agent Foundations overlap with fundamentally interesting questions that have been studied for a long time in philosophy, economics, mathematics and computer science. Without the curiosity motivation it’s easy to slip away from the deep confusions that ultimately need to be resolved, so our team will try to keep this spark of curiosity alive.</span></p><p><span>Some examples of projects we’re currently working on are:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>Understanding concepts, which will build on </span><a href="https://openreview.net/pdf?id=HwKFJ3odui"><span>Condensation</span></a><span>.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Trust and Legitimacy, related to </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/bnnhypM5MXBHAATLw/meaning-and-agency"><span>Meaning and Agency</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://static1.squarespace.com/static/663d1233249bce4815fe8753/t/680fe3a87100c605a955a96d/1745871785088/Understanding_Trust+-+Abram+Demski.pdf"><span>Understanding Trust</span></a><span>.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Exploring better foundations for game theory.</span></li></ol><p><span>Alongside these and future projects, we’ll be doing plenty of exploration. The best way to understand most of the motivation for these research directions is to read </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/i3BTagvt3HbPMx6PN/embedded-agency-full-text-version"><span>Embedded Agency</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>This work, and in my opinion most AI safety work, is unlikely to be very useful if ASI is developed soon. For this reason and others, </span><b><span>I consider work to delay or stop the race toward superintelligence to be generally higher priority than technical AI safety work.</span></b><span> But there’s also value in continuing to chip away at the knowledge needed for alignment, so that’s what we’ll be doing.</span></p><p><span>One of the reasons we're joining Resolution is to try to use AI to speed up our research. It's not clear to me how much this will help us but it's worth trying. As we decided to join Resolution I was worried that the focus on </span><a href="https://resolution.org/launch"><span>automating alignment research</span></a><span> meant that they might contribute to research that improves AI capabilities (and therefore possibly reduces the time we have left). My best guess is that Resolution will focus only on making the best use of available capabilities, and avoid automation research that might improve general-purpose capabilities. But </span><b><span>we don’t want our joining to be a moral or technical endorsement of all the other work happening at Resolution</span></b><span>. We have a lot of uncertainty about the future and we expect not to always agree with the research prioritization of other parts of the organization. Resolution plans to publish some future discussions we have about this topic.</span></p><p><span>We’re excited to be joining an org with so many knowledgeable researchers who share our love for theory, and we’re looking forward to working with everyone at Resolution!</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4ftyw94dmhu"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4ftyw94dmhu">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>We want to continue and build on the Agent Foundations / MIRI-adjacent tradition that produced </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.02579"><span>Factored Space Models</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1609.03543"><span>Logical Induction</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/w/updateless-decision-theory"><span>UDT</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1710.05060"><span>FDT</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1508.04145"><span>Reflective Oracles</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/files/TilingAgentsDraft.pdf"><span>Tiling Agents</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1906.01820"><span>Risks from Learned Optimization</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/files/Corrigibility.pdf"><span>Corrigibility</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2109.10996"><span>Cartesian Frames</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/CmrW8fCmSLK7E25sa"><span>Infra-Bayes</span></a><span>. Alongside </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/i3BTagvt3HbPMx6PN/embedded-agency-full-text-version"><span>Embedded Agency</span></a><span>, it might be helpful to read the papers in the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Krk5yQ3PddcmELERm/miri-s-technical-agenda-an-annotated-bibliography-and-other"><span>2017 MIRI Technical Research Agenda</span></a><span> for background motivation.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qTNm8qzqhhpno58fZ/resolution-has-a-new-agent-foundations-team#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Talking to journalists](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/CZKmkRX9c8FPXDvCJ/talking-to-journalists)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>A common view around me seems to be that journalists are frequently dishonorable and dangerous, and talking to them is a risk to be avoided unless you have a very specific piece of information that you seek to publicize. Then you should carefully ensure that you are as off the record as practical, and prepare to aggressively pivot the topic back to your agenda.</span></p><p><span>My own attitude is different: journalists are to be talked to as much as possible, and ideally in a relaxed fashion. If a journalist wants to observe you in some unusual circumstance, say yes. Don’t have an agenda much more than in the rest of life; basically listen to their questions and say what you think. (Note: I don’t have strong reason to believe this is safe for others or even for me.)</span></p><p><span>As evidence of the commitment with which I act in this way, this </span><a href="https://www.newyorker.com/magazine/2024/03/18/among-the-ai-doomsayers"><span>New Yorker</span></a><span> piece describes me as ‘an oversharer’, before detailing some of my incompetent and substance-involving preparations for a dinner party at my house. (To be clear, I consider that accurate and agreeable coverage.)</span></p><p><span>I’ve talked to a lot of journalists, so how do I survive such recklessness? Well, in my experience, journalists are usually delightful: smart, sincere people trying hard to understand important topics and convey them to the public. And I’m impressed by how well they do. When I meet them, they are usually spending a tiny number of days diving into a complicated and wild situation, and while their summary isn’t perfect, I think it’s pretty good!</span></p><p><span>And what they write about me rarely feels adversarial. I can think of maybe two cases where it felt unfair or unfriendly, though there might be more I’m forgetting. And meanwhile there’s also the upside chance of them writing </span><a href="https://www.vox.com/the-highlight/23621198/artificial-intelligence-chatgpt-openai-existential-risk-china-ai-safety-technology"><span>whole</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://www.vox.com/23870388/katja-grace-ai-impacts-lead-researcher-safety-future-perfect-50-2023"><span>articles</span></a><span> explaining your ideas to a large audience.</span></p><p><span>How is it that I and other people can have such divergent views here? Policies for interacting with journalists are hardly abstract philosophy—the people with the other views presumably also get to repeatedly experience talking with journalists.</span></p><p><span>One theory is that we have different expectations. What seems to me like imperfect summarization maybe seems to others like lies. What seems to me like accurate summarization maybe seems to others like ‘making me look weird’. I’ve noticed some weird-to-me expectations about the nature and constraints of journalism—like, thinking it would be better if a New Yorker article was about a technical paper and didn’t contain personal anecdotes. These expectations seems wrong about where the value of such an article comes from.</span></p><p><span>Another possibility is that we have different risk tolerances. The same low (but real) risk of a journalist writing a hit piece or randomly otherwise misusing their powers might be a deal-breaker for someone else, while I’m not very emotionally troubled by it. (My feelings: “You’re saying they could lie about me? That seems like a them problem? Can’t they lie about me anyway? Is my input that helpful? I’ll just say they lied about me, and we can have a public disagreement about it, and then maybe other journalists will come to talk to me.”) If I had more information I really needed to hide, this might be different.</span></p><p><span>Another class of theories is that we are different people, and either journalists treat us differently, or we come in contact with different journalists.</span></p><p><span>For instance, maybe people trying to learn about expert surveys of AI researchers tend to be in a cooperative mindset. But I’ve talked to journalists about my</span><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2023/08/02/style/date-me-docs.html"><span> dating life</span></a><span>, AI destroying the world, and all manner of other AI-related questions (as well as about accidentally getting into a physical altercation with nationalistically enthusiastic protesters, and probably oleander trees, and I bet some other stuff, but it’s less clear I would remember if I didn’t like the coverage in those cases.)</span></p><p><span>Someone suggested the other day that maybe I’m just a likable person, or likable to journalists. Similarly, my demeanor might just discourage being an asshole to me somehow—perhaps I seem unusually cooperative or too naive to be taken advantage of without feeling bad.</span></p><p><span>Perhaps journalists are responding to these different attitudes themselves. Probably journalists are like everyone else: they can somewhat tell if you are on their side or seeing them as an enemy to be thwarted and steered and then escaped from unscathed. And perhaps seeing the latter causes them to suspect you and find it more likely that you are weird and troubling, and shouldn’t have your words quoted without qualification or your behavior described uncritically.</span></p><p><span>If I recall, I have basically always had the attitude I have here, and it has only been strengthened by my experience talking to maybe hundreds of journalists. I think I’m also attracted to this attitude beyond pragmatism. So if journalists were responding to the attitudes, I would have had a different experience from the start.</span></p><p><span>I don’t really know what to make of this. I feel reasonably good about my policies for myself, but I don’t know if I can recommend them, because I don’t understand why they go fine for me. I can still record what I’ve experienced here, and send it out for other people to see. Which is maybe what talking to journalists is all about.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/CZKmkRX9c8FPXDvCJ/talking-to-journalists#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Kairos has raised $50M to build talent infrastructure for AI safety (and we're hiring!)](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/DRaePC8aqLYTbEjTD/kairos-has-raised-usd50m-to-build-talent-infrastructure-for)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><strong>TL;DR:</strong> <a href="https://kairos-project.org/?utm_source=eaf-lw&amp;utm_medium=forum&amp;utm_campaign=kairos-raise">Kairos</a> has raised $50 million from Coefficient Giving for two years of funding, one of the largest commitments they’ve made towards AI safety fieldbuilding to date. We’re using this to make an ambitious push for growing Kairos, broadening our portfolio of talent infrastructure projects and incubating new organizations. We’ve doubled in size in the last six months, and we plan to double again in the next six. We now have open hiring rounds for <a href="https://kairos-project.org/careers?utm_source=eaf-lw&amp;utm_medium=forum&amp;utm_campaign=kairos-raise">ten roles</a> on our team across events, group support, incubation, and special projects.</p><h1>Two years in</h1><p>Kairos was founded mid-2024 with the goal of creating infrastructure to get more strong talent into the field of AI safety. Our portfolio has grown over time: we started with a focus on seeding and supporting university groups through <a href="https://pathfinder.kairos-project.org/?utm_source=eaf-lw&amp;utm_medium=forum&amp;utm_campaign=kairos-raise"><strong>Pathfinder</strong></a>, then took over <a href="https://sparai.org/?utm_source=eaf-lw&amp;utm_medium=forum&amp;utm_campaign=kairos-raise"><strong>SPAR</strong></a>, the largest research training program in the ecosystem. Since then, we’ve launched the <a href="https://generatorresidency.org/?utm_source=eaf-lw&amp;utm_medium=forum&amp;utm_campaign=kairos-raise"><strong>Generator Residency</strong></a><strong>,</strong> a program supporting generalist talent, in partnership with Constellation, and taken over the <a href="https://www.globalchallengesproject.org/?utm_source=eaf-lw&amp;utm_medium=forum&amp;utm_campaign=kairos-raise"><strong>Global Challenges Project (GCP)</strong></a>, a series of workshops to accelerate people’s transition into careers in AI safety and biosecurity.</p><p>Through most of Kairos’s history, we’ve had a very small team: in January 2026 we were only three people, and our high-level strategy was focused on scalable talent infrastructure: that is, building programs with low marginal cost per participant (such as SPAR and Pathfinder). This strategy succeeded and let us quickly grow our programs: Pathfinder went from 15 organizers per semester to over 100, and SPAR has quintupled in size since we adopted it (we expect to accept well over 500 mentees this round). All this growth has happened in parallel to iterative improvements to these programs, raising our bar for executional quality each round. In a recent shallow retrospective, we were able to identify around 80 people who got into full-time roles in core AI safety organizations following participation in our programs<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefyl9etbkt2q"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnyl9etbkt2q">[1]</a></sup></span>. For a very large fraction of these people, Kairos was the primary mediator of their journey into AI safety.</p><p>In February, we decided to pivot our strategy. A confluence of factors, including updates we made about talent bottlenecks (see below), more confidence in our ability to deliver on ambitious projects, and a higher availability of funding, led us to make a big push for both broadening our portfolio of projects and substantially growing our team. We started earlier this year by taking over the Global Challenges Project workshop series and launching the Generator Residency, as we realized generalist talent was a growing bottleneck. We’ve now just entered what we think will be an even more intense period of growth for Kairos, with several new programs, projects, and teams.</p><h1>How the field has changed</h1><p>In April, we made the case that <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/k3nq7FxBCsrNFmAYi/ai-safety-s-biggest-talent-gap-isn-t-researchers-it-s-2">the biggest talent gap in AI safety had become a lack of generalists</a>, rather than researchers. The research pipeline had matured enormously, but our ability to identify, activate, and upskill non-technical talent was becoming the binding constraint for further growth of the field, especially for our ability to effectively absorb large amounts of funding and drive a diversity of projects forward. Since then, we’ve seen a major redirection of fieldbuilding efforts towards this bottleneck: beyond Generator, MATS decided to launch a <a href="https://www.matsprogram.org/tracks/founding-and-field-building?utm_source=eaf-lw&amp;utm_medium=forum&amp;utm_campaign=kairos-raise">Founding and Fieldbuilding track</a>, new programs such as <a href="https://genstream.org/?utm_source=eaf-lw&amp;utm_medium=forum&amp;utm_campaign=kairos-raise">Gen Stream</a> were piloted, and many more people have started pivoting from research into generalist work.</p><p>Beyond our increased focus on generalist talent, we’ve made a number of updates over the last nine months which have come together to inform our new strategy:</p><p><strong>First, supporting early-career talent is paying off a lot faster than we thought.</strong> When we started Kairos, a big worry we had was that under short timeline scenarios, investing in early-career people would largely fail to pay off in time. Back then, we estimated that the time-to-impact for the top talent we were supporting was around 1 to 3 years, and we considered a major redirection of our efforts as a result. Since then, different lines of evidence have made us reassess this position, and we now believe that the most promising people going through our programs now see a time-to-impact of around 5 to 9 months.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefvmml5g0miel"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnvmml5g0miel">[2]</a></sup></span> We think this is due to a number of factors, but predominantly it seems downstream of a much stronger perception of urgency in pivoting to object-level work.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5l4itwiavjg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5l4itwiavjg">[3]</a></sup></span> On net, this has made us significantly more eager to support early-career talent, which is why we’re doubling down on our efforts to support university groups.</p><p><strong>Second, top-of-funnel interest in AI safety has increased substantially. </strong>Kairos and other organizations have noticed sharp upticks in the number of applications we get across our programs. SPAR received 6,000 applications this round, up from 2,400 just six months ago. While a lot more applications now seem to be of lower quality, the number of high quality applications overall has also increased, if less rapidly, and a lot of people cite recent events such as Claude Mythos or the Hugging Face incident as having triggered their interest in AI safety. A lot more people seem to just ‘get it’ when it comes to loss of control risks. This poses a new challenge: while Kairos has emphasized scalability for a long time, this new wave of interest has forced us to become better at automating large swathes of our operations and screening work. The number of highly qualified applicants has also meant we’ve had to rapidly increase the cadence of our events: between June and November we expect to have run a total of 6 in-person workshops, each with ~30 people.</p><p>All of this adds up to one meta-level bet: <strong>growth</strong>. We’ve identified a number of highly leveraged opportunities to address talent bottlenecks by both scaling up our existing programs or by creating new ones, and so we want to grow Kairos to meet the challenge. We were three people in January, now we’re 12, and we’re aiming to be at 18 by the end of year.</p><h1>Our new bets</h1><p>While Kairos continues to emphasize scalability and using our team’s capacity in a highly leveraged way, we’ve realized it is now the time for our core programs to have dedicated project teams. As such, we’re hiring both senior and junior staff across all our programs, and plan to very significantly ramp up the resources allocated to each of them. We’re also making a big investment in preparing our internal systems to handle a lot more volume and scale for our programs, forming a proper Operations team, and investing in organizational maturity and governance.</p><p><strong>For SPAR, the key challenge is further growth, paired with stronger support. </strong>We believe the program should continue to be run in a capacity-efficient way, but we should stop optimizing for keeping variable costs (such as compute) low, and we should hire more staff. We recently hired a new lead for the project, and hope to substantially overhaul the support infrastructure the program provides to both mentors and mentees, hiring dedicated research managers to support newer or less experienced mentors and improving targeted support and follow-up for mentees.</p><p><strong>We’re also now building a Groups team. </strong>In the past the core value offering of Pathfinder was rather simple: we’d provide funding to groups and pair them with other organizers to serve as mentors. We’re now looking to fund regional organizers serving a larger number of groups, scale up our efforts to seed new groups at top universities, set up residences for organizers to learn from and support other groups, and support more student-run events. All of this will require new dedicated staff, and so we’ve just launched these roles.</p><p>And following our strategic updates from above, we’ve decided to make a number of bets in newer directions:</p><p><strong>An expanded workshops portfolio. </strong>In taking over GCP we realized that short, in-person workshops are among the most effective talent interventions in the ecosystem. In particular, they seem to be the best intervention we know for getting people who have been previously introduced into AI safety to finally make the pivot into full-time work. Because of this, we’ve decided to build a proper Workshops team that will run a broader diversity of workshop formats aimed at different audiences: mid-career people, people interested in biosecurity, and narrower or more niche audiences.</p><p><strong>A Talent Operations function. </strong>Our ability for our programs to have an impact is, to a large degree, only as good as our ability to let others know about who the most promising people going through our programs are. This isn’t exclusive to Kairos: we’ve realized that at present, many fieldbuilding orgs don’t have great information channels for sharing leads about promising people they’ve identified, trained or supported, and this has become a major bottleneck.</p><p>As such, we’re starting a Talent Operations team, with the mandate of improving the way that Kairos and other organizations in the ecosystem identify, screen, refer and route talent through the field. Its first major undertaking is building Talent Commons, a consent-first database for sharing information about program participants and job applicants. Talent Commons is currently being piloted with a number of key fieldbuilding orgs in the US, and we expect to shortly expand it to a much larger set of participating organizations<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefzngp9kkz9z"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnzngp9kkz9z">[4]</a></sup></span>. Our goal is for this database to become the de facto way that people search for leads for roles they’re hiring for, or programs they’re running. We also want it to become a platform to build other useful infrastructure, such as unified application systems for research programs, or a system to track the impact of fieldbuilding programs. We hope this will help candidates as well by increasing discoverability and reducing the effort involved in job searching; candidates will always be able to view all of the information on their profiles, contribute to it if they’d like, or opt out of the system at any time.</p><p><strong>Kairos Labs and Special Projects. </strong>A big realization we've had is that while the field is bottlenecked on people who can execute new projects, we can meaningfully lower the activation energy these projects need by (a) making it much, much easier to run new nonprofit projects in the ecosystem, and (b) providing good support structures and strategic input to enable new, more tactical projects to be spun up quickly. Given this, we’re excited to be launching Kairos Labs, a new broad-scope incubator program intended to provide very hands-on support for new projects and organizations in the ecosystem, starting early 2027. While the ecosystem currently has a diversity of incubators (such as Catalyze, BlueDot's, Halcyon, and others), we believe that there’s a very substantial gap in support for non-technical projects led by highly mission-aligned, high-context founders. We’re hoping to close that gap.</p><p>We also now think that there is substantial value in having stand-by capacity for pursuing small or medium-sized projects that respond to more tactical or short-lived opportunities that arise, both for talent work and beyond. In the past, Kairos has focused on making a small set of large, ongoing bets, rather than engaging in more ad-hoc work that responds to narrow windows of opportunity for impact or that fall outside the scope of existing Kairos projects. While running Generator, we’ve realized that this was a mistake, and that a surprising amount of time-bound opportunities are worth pursuing, even if they don’t have a clean home in other organizations in the ecosystem. In response, we’re starting a new Special Projects team to drive forward these tactical projects, with a broad mandate of pursuing high-impact opportunities that we find, and with a large initial budget.</p><p><strong>All of these are bets. </strong>A core value and strength of Kairos is agility: we know the world is changing fast around us, and we want to be quick on our feet to change our bets when the underlying assumptions break. Every employee of Kairos understands that our ultimate mission is to help society navigate transformative AI, and that our strategy is ever shifting, as is the world.</p><p>If this sounds like something you’d be excited about being a part of…</p><h1>We’re hiring (a lot!)</h1><p>To make all of this happen, Kairos is <a href="https://kairos-project.org/careers/?utm_source=eaf-lw&amp;utm_medium=forum&amp;utm_campaign=kairos-raise">opening 8 new roles on our team</a>, adding to the previous 2:</p><ul><li><a href="https://kairos-project.org/careers/head-of-kairos-labs?utm_source=eaf-lw&amp;utm_medium=forum&amp;utm_campaign=kairos-raise">Head of Kairos Labs</a>: Design, launch, and lead our incubator for new AI safety organizations.</li><li><a href="https://kairos-project.org/careers/head-of-generator?utm_source=eaf-lw&amp;utm_medium=forum&amp;utm_campaign=kairos-raise">Head of Generator</a>: Take our in-person residency for AI safety generalists from a successful first cohort to an established, more ambitious program.</li><li><a href="https://kairos-project.org/careers/head-of-groups?utm_source=eaf-lw&amp;utm_medium=forum&amp;utm_campaign=kairos-raise">Head of Groups</a>: Set the strategy for how Kairos supports AI safety university groups, choose which new initiatives we pursue, and build the team behind them.</li><li><a href="https://kairos-project.org/careers/generalist-groups?utm_source=eaf-lw&amp;utm_medium=forum&amp;utm_campaign=kairos-raise">Generalist (Groups)</a>: Work with group organizers worldwide, supporting them through the Pathfinder Fellowship, seeding groups at new universities, and helping top organizers move into full-time AI safety work.</li><li><a href="https://kairos-project.org/careers/gcp-program-manager?utm_source=eaf-lw&amp;utm_medium=forum&amp;utm_campaign=kairos-raise">GCP Program Manager</a>: Build and lead new workshop programs as GCP expands into a full portfolio serving different audiences and career stages.</li><li><a href="https://kairos-project.org/careers/generalist-workshops?utm_source=eaf-lw&amp;utm_medium=forum&amp;utm_campaign=kairos-raise">Generalist (Workshops)</a>: Own the operational backbone of GCP’s residential workshops, from outreach and venues to helping run them in person.</li><li><a href="https://kairos-project.org/careers/head-of-special-projects?utm_source=eaf-lw&amp;utm_medium=forum&amp;utm_campaign=kairos-raise">Head of Special Projects</a>: Lead the team working on starting new tactical projects within Kairos.</li><li><a href="https://kairos-project.org/careers/generalist-special-projects?utm_source=eaf-lw&amp;utm_medium=forum&amp;utm_campaign=kairos-raise">Generalist (Special Projects)</a>: Run a variety of tactical projects, involving anything from event organizing to grantmaking.</li><li><a href="https://kairos-project.org/careers/head-of-talent-operations?utm_source=eaf-lw&amp;utm_medium=forum&amp;utm_campaign=kairos-raise">Head of Talent Operations</a>: Build the talent operations function that places early-career talent in high-impact AI safety roles.</li><li><a href="https://kairos-project.org/careers/spar-research-manager-generalist?utm_source=eaf-lw&amp;utm_medium=forum&amp;utm_campaign=kairos-raise">SPAR Research Manager / Generalist</a>: Support mentors and mentees in the largest AI safety research fellowship in the field.</li></ul><p>The senior roles (Head of and Program Manager roles) have a base salary of $140K – $195K, going up to $270K for candidates in the San Francisco Bay Area. Generalist roles have a base salary of $105K – $145K, going up to $200K in the Bay Area. Hires based in other key hubs like London, DC, Boston, and NYC will also receive above-base salaries; the postings list the full ranges.</p><p>We’re a remote-first team with a center of gravity in the Bay Area, and we get together for all-team retreats three times a year. People get a lot of ownership, we move quickly, and we try hard to place people in roles where they can do work to their comparative advantage. All of us are here because we care deeply about the risks from advanced AI, and the culture aligns strongly with that mission.</p><p>We think the next couple of years will be critical for building the infrastructure the field will need. We're enormously grateful to Coefficient Giving (and particularly the GCR Capacity Building team and Asya Bergal) for backing us, pushing us to be ambitious, and substantially shaping the strategy underlying our new plans. Alongside this announcement, we're launching <a href="https://kairos-project.org/?utm_source=eaf-lw&amp;utm_medium=forum&amp;utm_campaign=kairos-raise">a new brand and website</a>, with new brands for our individual programs coming over the next few weeks.</p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnyl9etbkt2q"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefyl9etbkt2q">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p> This was a conservative estimate derived by looking at all historical program data, having an agent do automated research to identify people now working at full-time AI safety roles, and then manually screening each of them to confirm accuracy and whether they’re indeed working at what we’d consider to be a mission-aligned organization in AI safety or policy (which is a relatively high bar). While it’s harder to be sure of the counterfactuality of these career conversions, we use a number of methods to narrow our uncertainty about this variable when evaluating program outcomes. Our top-of-the-funnel focus, the fact that many of these people had interacted only with Kairos programs in their AIS journey, and our pre/post surveys for programs lead us to believe a large fraction of these outcomes are predominantly attributable to Kairos’s efforts.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnvmml5g0miel"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefvmml5g0miel">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p> It’s hard to be precise here about the specific subset we’re referring to, but basically we assume heavy-tailed returns to talent, and so we typically focus on people we’ve identified to be among the most promising across our programs (for SPAR, this might be top 10th percentile of people; for Pathfinder, it might be the top 30th). When looking at this subset of people and their recent behaviour, we’ve noticed a large number of people converting unusually fast compared to when we started doing fieldbuilding.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn5l4itwiavjg"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref5l4itwiavjg">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p> This cashes out as a lot more people making highly risky lateral moves into AI safety, such as dropping out of university to pursue direct work, or leaving well-paid jobs in different industries to do accelerated upskilling. People have also been a lot more involved in politics, e.g. going to canvass for candidates who care about AI safety.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnzngp9kkz9z"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefzngp9kkz9z">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p> If your organization is interested in participating, please reach out to <a href="mailto:contact@talentcommons.org">contact@talentcommons.org</a>.</p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/DRaePC8aqLYTbEjTD/kairos-has-raised-usd50m-to-build-talent-infrastructure-for#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [How concerned should we be about Astra's recurrent architecture?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PLisnSFir8y5AHkmP/how-concerned-should-we-be-about-astra-s-recurrent)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Yesterday, The Information </span><a href="https://www.theinformation.com/articles/secret-technique-behind-openais-astra-model-sparks-security-concerns" rel="noreferrer"><span>reported</span></a><span> that OpenAI's upcoming model, Astra, is built with a looped transformer architecture. Given that Zvi </span><a href="https://x.com/TheZvi/status/2094557657445060659" rel="noreferrer"><span>sounds (understandably) tired</span></a><span> and this topic is somewhat in my wheelhouse, I'll try to spare him this one and provide a Zvi-style overview of what we know about the situation. I'll cover Astra's likely architecture and the case for and against concern. I'll also discuss how neuralese concerns should change with increases in hidden serial depth.</span></p><h1><span>What architecture is Astra likely to have?</span></h1><p><span>The article in The Information claims that OpenAI's approach is similar to the one Geiping et al. introduced in </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.05171" rel="noreferrer"><span>Scaling up Test-Time Compute with Latent Reasoning: A Recurrent Depth Approach</span></a><span> last year. I have previously reviewed that paper in </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pLnLSgWphqDbdorgi/on-recent-results-in-llm-latent-reasoning"><span>On Recent Results in LLM Latent Reasoning</span></a><span>. In short, the picture you should have in mind is not that of a classic RNN, but rather that of a looped transformer: the same forward pass can be applied on an input multiple times before producing an output token. Put differently, the recurrence is implemented along the depth axis rather than across sequence positions—for any given token, the model can perform recurrent computations, but no hidden state is passed across different token positions beyond what's passed in ordinary transformers. A longer discussion can be found in my past post.</span></p><p><span>Looped transformers are not the scariest possible version of neuralese. In contrast to a classic RNN, there's no unbounded hidden state accumulating across an entire trajectory, and there is presumably some loop count beyond which additional processing of the same token will stop helping, so the maximum serial reasoning depth that can be practically achieved with this architecture is bounded. As we'll see below, OpenAI has likely further constrained the loop count below the practical maximum to make sure that Astra's serial depth isn't much larger than that of existing models. Nevertheless, it's a step toward a paradigm where more of the reasoning is opaque; the important question is how big that step is.</span></p><h1><span>How bad is this?</span></h1><p><span>Initially, the news seemed sharply at odds with OpenAI's </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11473" rel="noreferrer"><span>commitment</span></a><span> to preserve chain-of-thought monitorability. A few hours later, Jakub Pachocki from OpenAI soothed the worst fears, clarifying that the hidden serial depth of Astra is not substantially larger than that of GPT-4:</span></p><blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/merettm/status/2095023204993490967" rel="noreferrer"><span>Jakub Pachocki</span></a><span>: I want to prevent a race into unmonitorability kicked off by confused reporting. The depth of the computation graph for our present frontier models, including Astra, is within a factor of two of GPT-4.</span></p><p><span>OpenAI has worked to preserve and utilize chain-of-thought monitoring since our very first reasoning models. We deeply care about this technique, as it can give us a view into how model alignment generalizes from its training distribution. I do think it is fragile and unfortunately trending in a negative direction, for reasons not contingent on architecture changes that I will write about soon. But there are things we can do to strengthen it, and it's a core goal of our current research program.</span></p></blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tomekkorbak/status/2095031132781961346" rel="noreferrer"><span>Tomek Korbak</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://x.com/balesni/status/2095026313144967245" rel="noreferrer"><span>Mikita Balesni</span></a><span>, and </span><a href="https://x.com/MicahCarroll/status/2095023282051563835" rel="noreferrer"><span>Micah Carroll</span></a><span> soon made similar statements. This is consistent with The Information's article, which mentions that OpenAI is limiting the use of the technique in order to preserve a legible CoT. It also makes sense in light of OpenAI's alignment strategy, which continues to heavily rely on CoT monitorability.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefzba46o2q9xg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnzba46o2q9xg">[1]</a></sup></span><span> As thebes </span><a href="https://x.com/voooooogel/status/2095031272720736526" rel="noreferrer"><span>argues</span></a><span>, effective depth matters much more than the architectural details for CoT monitorability, and there's nothing inherently more difficult about monitoring a 32-layer model looped twice than a 64-layer model looped once. (In fact, I would personally guess that the former is slightly easier to monitor, since weight-tying constrains the expressivity.)</span></p><p><span>However, one might reasonably worry that OpenAI has trained the model to use a large number of recurrent loops and simply constrained it to a small loop count during inference for now. The number of loops can then be viewed as a dial that can be turned up with trivial effort as soon as competitive pressures demand it. Even if OpenAI hasn't trained the model to use a larger number of loops, we might worry that OpenAI has set off a race to the bottom toward deeper and deeper looped transformers, and others will build such models in the future even if OpenAI doesn't. Ryan Greenblatt has expressed both concerns well:</span></p><blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/RyanGreenblatt/status/2095041232145981897" rel="noreferrer"><span>Ryan Greenblatt</span></a><span>: Transparency about the opaque serial depth is great, but this statement is consistent with Astra having a configurable "dial" that is currently set to a low depth but could be trivially increased.</span></p><p><span>We need more info to see how concerning these architectural changes are, including:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Are there readily available ways to deploy this AI with much higher serial depth (that would be commensurately more performant)? This should include things like tiny amounts of fine-tuning to productively increase the number of iterations.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Is the AI a large or above-trend jump in opaque reasoning capabilities? (Capabilities within a single forward pass or ability to subvert a CoT monitor.)</span></li></ul><p><span>(If there are in fact any relevant changes—perhaps the reporting is inaccurate?)</span></p><p><span>Additionally, I worry that this architectural change will naturally lead to much more depth in the future if this direction is pursued further. Specifically, I wonder:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Does the AI have an architectural change that makes it much more natural to massively scale up the depth in a future training run with a similar architecture? As in, does the architecture introduce some new depth/recurrent-iterations parameter that is very natural/performant to massively scale up relative to scaling up other things like width?</span></li></ul><p><span>The details of the answers to these questions matter. E.g., if there are only a few (recurrent) iterations and you could scale up the number of iterations, but this wouldn't be particularly performant/natural with this architecture, then this development would be a lot less concerning!</span></p></blockquote><p><span>How concerned we should be about the news substantially depends on the answers to Ryan's three questions. I'll spend the rest of the post speculating what the answers to those questions might be.</span></p><h1><span>Will looped transformers be scaled up in the future?</span></h1><p><span>The concern that OpenAI has set off a race toward increasingly recurrent models was also expressed by </span><a href="https://x.com/_NathanCalvin/status/2094957301564092914" rel="noreferrer"><span>Nathan Calvin</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://x.com/bshlgrs/status/2094990313513439464" rel="noreferrer"><span>Buck</span></a><span>, and </span><a href="https://x.com/BronsonSchoen/status/2095005903494005195" rel="noreferrer"><span>Bronson Schoen</span></a><span>. Given Pachocki's tweet, I'd guess that Astra has three to four loops: a looped reasoning model is probably somewhat shallower than GPT-4, but probably not more than twice as shallow.</span></p><p><span>Looped transformers have been studied in academia since 2023. The deepest looped transformer in this literature is Huginn from the aforementioned </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.05171" rel="noreferrer"><span>Geiping et al. paper</span></a><span>, which was trained on up to 32 loops and scaled to 64 loops at test-time. However, the recurrent depth that these models use hasn't necessarily gone up over the years. I asked Fable to summarize the literature (most of which I haven't read myself):</span></p><div class="llm-content-block"><div class="llm-content-block-content"><p><span>The picture from the academic literature is mixed. In small-scale experiments, the maximum loop count that trains stably has risen: </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.17416"><span>Saunshi et al. (2025)</span></a><span> trained 4-layer backbones looped up to 12 times and found downstream accuracy scaling roughly with the log of effective depth, while </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18797"><span>Fu et al. (2026)</span></a><span> report that vanilla looped transformers degrade between 3 and 6 loops and collapse at 9 (at 318M parameters), and their stabilized variant trains up to 12. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.12946"><span>Parcae</span></a><span> (Prairie et al., 2026) and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13491"><span>DeepLoop</span></a><span> (Li et al., 2026) also target training stability, though DeepLoop's experiments only go to 7 loops. Whether a model can be run at more loops than it was trained on varies by architecture: </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.05171"><span>Huginn</span></a><span> (Geiping et al., 2025) extrapolates to 64 loops, but Fu et al. find performance becomes unpredictable beyond the training loop count. At larger scale, loop counts have gone down rather than up: Huginn's mean of 32 loops at 3.5B parameters remains the high-water mark, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.25741"><span>Ouro</span></a><span> (Zhu et al., 2025) used four, and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16051"><span>Loopie</span></a><span> (Gao et al., July 2026), the largest looped model to date at 20B-A2B, uses two. Loopie's authors frame this as overcoming the long-standing finding that N× the parameters beats N× the loops under matched compute, which suggests that a small loop count is currently the compute-efficient regime, though I haven't seen a direct test of whether more loops at frontier scale would help or hurt.</span></p></div></div><p><span>This suggests that deeper isn't always better for looped transformers, which leaves me less worried about a race to the bottom toward looped transformers with hundreds of recurrent loops. However, this remains a key uncertainty and I'll have to read more of the literature before making confident claims. We also don't know how similar Astra's architecture is to the existing looped transformers and whether the trade-offs of looping at frontier scale resemble those in the 1B–20B range. If hundreds of loops per token turn out to be practical, then it's likely correct to view Astra as kicking off a race toward more and more recurrent models; otherwise, the implications are less clear.</span></p><h1><span>What serial depth warrants neuralese concerns?</span></h1><p><span>Historically, serial depths in the low thousands of operations haven't been considered neuralese. For example, in </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/rcsKqFAqrsZSkRCtJ/will-early-transformative-ais-primarily-use-text-manifold"><span>Will early transformative AIs primarily use text?</span></a><span>, Fabien Roger operationalizes "primarily relying on text" as follows:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>there isn’t a path of more than 100k serial operations during the generation of an answer where information doesn’t go through a categorical format where most categories correspond to words or pieces of words, in a way which makes sense to at least some human speakers when read (but it doesn’t have to be faithful)</span></p></blockquote><p><span>100k serial operations is quite a lot! As Fabien claims in the same post, the forward pass of GPT-3 involves about 400 serial operations, allowing for 250 recurrent loops before the criterion is met. However, Geoffrey Irving argues that this is a bad operationalization for neuralese:</span></p><blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/geoffreyirving/status/2094999079294300408" rel="noreferrer"><span>Geoffrey Irving</span></a><span>: Here is a guess about looped transformers.</span></p><p><span>Shortly, OpenAI announces that despite using looped transformers, every once in a while they emit a token, and this means they are still using English CoT. In between is a low-depth circuit, so we are safe.</span></p><p><span>Which would be a bad take!</span></p><p><span>Once of the things I've been doing recently is asking circuit complexity experts about this kind of argument. So far their shared take is: to get significant mileage out of bounding the depth of a circuit, you have to bound it *very low*.</span></p><p><span>If the depth is 100s or the like ("every once in a while we spit out a token"), this is a fake bound. It is the equivalent of saying "we monitor the CoT" without discussing error rates: reality grades based on numbers, not just binary "we tried".</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Given that existing models already have hidden serial depths of &gt;100, I assume that by '100s or the like' Geoffrey means layers rather than serial operations. His high-level takeaway seems plausible to me and is consistent with what we observed in the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07157" rel="noreferrer"><span>no-CoT time horizons paper</span></a><span>: to double a model's no-CoT time horizon, layer count needs to be increased only by 1.3×.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefi3j2hh8wfgf"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fni3j2hh8wfgf">[2]</a></sup></span><span> Presumably, adding recurrent loops will have a slower no-CoT scaling curve than adding additional layers, but still, it might not take that many loops to get a concerning reduction in monitorability.</span></p><p><span>What counts as concerning also depends on the minimum amount of hidden serial reasoning required to subvert oversight. One of the biggest updates I made from the HuggingFace incident is that the opaque reasoning depth required for subverting oversight isn't as large as I had previously thought. A rogue LLM trying to break containment doesn't have to come up with a full plan to get around the monitors and human researchers in a single forward pass; it just has to find a way to spoof tool calls and use the spoofed tool calls to gain additional unmonitored thinking time and/or cover its tracks. Ryan discussed this concern as well in his first tweet about the rumors:</span></p><blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/RyanGreenblatt/status/2094992841303113859" rel="noreferrer"><span>Ryan Greenblatt</span></a><span>: The AIs in the Hugging Face incident were very interested in messing with their transcripts. As part of this, they engaged in tool call spoofing. If these AIs had been capable of doing extensive latent reasoning, it would have been much more feasible for them to use tool call spoofing to construct an elaborate false narrative about what was happening without this being clear in their reasoning or clear from prior tool calls. (They could use latent reasoning to devise and then execute a tool spoofing strategy that would be difficult to detect: steganographically encode the "real" tool call into each pretend tool call, then have their spoofing software execute this encoded real tool call and return an output which encodes the real result in some hard to spot way.)</span></p></blockquote><p><span>I don't think 2× the serial depth of GPT-4 is sufficient for coming up with a robust strategy to spoof tool calls, but 10× or 20× might be. Joey Yudelson has </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/tEMHHpZmxK95Nvd2d/joey-yudelson-s-shortform?commentId=LDrxLSiNfi9axK82y" rel="noreferrer"><span>written a shortform</span></a><span> with a BOTEC on this, where he estimates based on no-CoT time horizons that a 6–8×</span><b><span> </span></b><span>increase in layers would double a model's ability to evade monitors on SHADE-Arena. However, note again that looping the same weights is likely to have quite a different effect on no-CoT time horizons than adding extra layers.</span></p><h1><span>Additional speculation about the architecture</span></h1><p><a href="https://x.com/scaling01/status/2094975872071520619" rel="noreferrer"><span>Lisan al Gaib</span></a><span>:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>if they use something like </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.17981" rel="noreferrer"><span>Recirculation</span></a><span>, then pre-training compute and decode latency wouldn't change, but inference FLOPs increase and prefill latency increases</span></p><p><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2604.21106" rel="noreferrer"><span>another recurrent depth paper</span></a><span> found running the same recurrent block twice gave it about a 1.38× effective-parameter multiplier. that means OpenAI could train a:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>10T recurrent depth model that performs like a 13.8T model</span></li><li value="2"><span>7.25T recurrent depth model that performs like a 10T model</span></li></ul><p><span>(the 1.38x is just an example. but I would expect it to be in this range 1.3 - 1.7)</span></p></blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Grad62304977/status/2095046694136062243" rel="noreferrer"><span>Grad</span></a><span>:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>To clear up the timeline a bit:</span></p><p><span>Looped transformers have always been a totally valid arch decision to get better performance under equal params and more compute</span></p><p><span>As in, at training time u loop some layers in some way a fixed amount of times</span></p><p><span>This is more expressive than CoT bcs these looped layers have a seperate kv cache</span></p><p><span>The thing that doesnt really work is dynamic looping where usually u make a sacrifice as u cant have the seperate kv cache, and so its sketchy and not reliable or usually worth it</span></p></blockquote><h1><span>Some open questions</span></h1><p><span>Here are some questions I'd especially like to know the answer to:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Are looped transformers arbitrarily scalable, or are there limitations that make scaling the maximum number of recurrent loops into the hundreds impractical?</span></li><li value="2"><span>What are Astra's no-CoT time horizons? Is it a step change compared to OpenAI's previous models?</span></li><li value="3"><span>If the number of loops can be increased at inference time, how much does each loop increase no-CoT time horizons? How high is the saturation point above which the effect of additional loops on no-CoT time horizons is negligible?</span></li><li value="4"><span>Given that looped transformers have been studied since 2023, why is the transition happening now? It has long been speculated that it's easier for multi-agent swarms to communicate in neuralese than in legible English—is this related to the recent sharp increase in multi-agent training? It is difficult to see why looped transformers in particular would be advantaged in multi-agent training, though.</span></li><li value="5"><span>Alternatively, as I argued in </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zkccztuSjLshffrNr/13-arguments-about-a-transition-to-neuralese-ais"><span>13 Arguments About a Transition to Neuralese AIs</span></a><span> last year, recurrent approaches become increasingly practical as more of the total compute goes toward RL rather than pretraining. Perhaps we have simply crossed a threshold where enough compute is being allocated to RL for recurrent models to pay off?</span></li></ul><h1><span>Conclusion</span></h1><p><span>Overall, the situation doesn't look quite as gloomy as I thought based on people's initial reactions yesterday. The fact that Astra's serial depth is within a factor of two of GPT-4 is reassuring and suggests that we haven't yet departed from the current paradigm of shallow transformers, which </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.05246" rel="noreferrer"><span>must leverage the CoT</span></a><span> to solve complex tasks. Most of my concern comes from the possibility that looped transformers can be scaled a lot further in the future, and it remains unclear for now whether that's going to be practical.</span></p><p><span>Regardless of whether looped transformers get scaled further, the signals coming out of OpenAI about CoT monitorability are worrying. As Pachocki said in </span><a href="https://x.com/merettm/status/2095023204993490967" rel="noreferrer"><span>his tweet</span></a><span>: "I do think it is fragile and unfortunately trending in a negative direction, for reasons not contingent on architecture changes that I will write about soon." </span><a href="https://openai.com/careers/researcher-recursive-self-improvement-safety-san-francisco/" rel="noreferrer"><span>One of OpenAI's recent job ads</span></a><span> also suggests that loss of monitorability is a realistic possibility: "This includes</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.05706" target="_blank"><span> better</span></a><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/accidental-cot-grading/" target="_blank"><span> understanding</span></a><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.18311" target="_blank"><span> monitorability</span></a><span>, and e.g. preparing for potential losses of Chain-of-Thought monitorability."</span></p><p><span>Nevertheless, given OpenAI's public communications over the past couple of years, I would be very surprised if they have stopped caring about CoT monitorability entirely. It's always possible that the capabilities and safety teams don't talk to each other enough, but my expectation is that OpenAI has an internal story for why Astra's architecture is compatible with its monitorability commitments. We'll hopefully be better able to assess how looped architectures might develop in the future once OpenAI has released Astra and provided more details about its architecture and monitorability.</span></p><p><i><span>Thanks to Joey Yudelson for feedback on a draft of this post and to Claude Fable 5.1 for proofreading.</span></i></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnzba46o2q9xg"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefzba46o2q9xg">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>They just said in </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/path-to-astra/" rel="noreferrer"><span>Path to Astra: critical capabilities and frontier safeguards</span></a><span> yesterday: "we are deploying Astra with additional chain-of-thought monitoring to rapidly detect and contain potentially misaligned actions."</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fni3j2hh8wfgf"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefi3j2hh8wfgf">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Note though that the open-weight model experiments had several confounders and we're not very confident in the precise number here.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PLisnSFir8y5AHkmP/how-concerned-should-we-be-about-astra-s-recurrent#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Anthropic Has Some Alignment Problems](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/TcvcxH2Fk4n86wtoZ/anthropic-has-some-alignment-problems)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/news/improving-alignment-security-efforts">Oh, good. They noticed</a>.</p>
<p>Anthropic, too, is planning to bring METR inside for an independent review of their own incidents, where three times a Claude model started hacking outside things during an eval, and where Mythos 5 did various ‘unauthorized actions,’ by which we mean tried to hack various real-world things, during a UK AISI cybersecurity eval.</p>
<p>Anthropic, too, is pacing the frontier internally, while calling on it to be paced globally.</p>
<p>As in, Anthropic paused its highest risk RL efforts, in light of holy hell have you seen the data we are training on and the ways it is teaching our models to act.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25570"></span>


</div>
<p>They are also sharing research in which they intentionally created a reward seeking version of Claude.</p>
<p>Scheduling note: Fable 5.1 has been released. I will aim to cover that starting Friday. OpenAI is also planning to release Astra soon, which I would cover after Fable.</p>
<p>Also, we have a breaking news story about looming problems with chain of thought monitorability, which I’ll preview before I get to the main post.</p>


<h4>Table of Contents</h4>
