### [Don’t be the vitamin B guy](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ZHJvdkQENxyfzhpCj/don-t-be-the-vitamin-b-guy)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span>Towards scientific rigor for decentralized science.</span></i></p><p><span>When I was eleven years old, I watched my favorite YouTuber surgically implant a magnet into his finger.</span></p><p><span>In the </span><a href="https://archive.org/details/CodysLab_me/20140227-Diy+Magnetic+Implant.mp4" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>since-deleted video</span></a><span>, beloved mad scientist </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cody_Reeder" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Cody Reeder</span></a><span> covered a neodymium magnet with gold using a homemade electroplating rig. Then he cut open his finger, inserted the magnet, and sutured the wound closed with horsehair he had taken from his own horse.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="frame_0137.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1788314001/lexical_client_uploads/au1hkq1mu3tngcnew5zr.png" /><figcaption><p><span>Cody polishes the magnet in preparation for surgery. The beaker contains a gold cyanide solution used to electroplate a thin bioinert coating onto the magnet. Cody’sLab </span><a href="https://www.popularmechanics.com/science/a23416/mad-scientist-drinks-cyanide-on-camera/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>went viral in 2016</span></a><span> for drinking a small dose of cyanide </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=i6hOVhQQ9hI" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>on camera</span></a><span>.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>The footage is an uncomfortable watch for medical professionals and squeamish laymen alike. The scalpel was chipped, there was no anesthetic, and at one point, Cody dips a mechanical pencil in alcohol and uses its tip to push the magnet deeper into the incision. Being eleven, I thought it was badass.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefpx47fskrbj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnpx47fskrbj">[1]</a></sup></span><span> And yet, after the juvenile enthusiasm faded, I was left unsettled, not by the blood or the questionable sterile technique, but by a newfound resentment at the lack of a sense I never had. A sense that wasn’t even human. Like a fish learning about water, I woke to the injustice of my biological limitations, and since then, I’ve considered myself an aspiring biohacker.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4zdtl1wia9d"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4zdtl1wia9d">[2]</a></sup></span></p><blockquote><p><span>Back when I had [a magnet] in the first time […] I became very fond of being able to tell when things were magnetic, you know, feel electricity going through wires without touching them, or determine if an object is metallic iron or not. And now that the magnet is gone, I actually feel blind. […] It’s hard to explain, but I really want the magnet back.</span></p><p><i><span>From the Diy Magnetic Implant video by Cody’sLab.</span></i></p></blockquote><p><span>It wasn’t until college that I attended my first biohacking event in meatspace: Grindfest.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ZHJvdkQENxyfzhpCj/qxfvjtn9riflrpppfi4x" /><figcaption><p><span>Grindfest attendee </span><a href="https://asyaplugged.in/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Asya</span></a><span> gave me a “cyborg manicure” consisting of an LED and magnet under clear nail polish, pictured above. The magnet mimics the function of an implanted one, but with significantly less sensitivity.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>With an emphasis on the “meat.” I’ve never been anywhere that made me so acutely aware of the fact that I am made of it.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefjjhwjuwdy3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnjjhwjuwdy3">[3]</a></sup></span><span> It is one thing to browse pictures and videos of unusual body mods, another to meet a biohacker in the altered flesh and see the LEDs shining from under her skin or from the iris of his </span><a href="https://www.instagram.com/bsmachinist/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>prosthetic eye</span></a><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref9da9ypvd7e"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn9da9ypvd7e">[4]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>On the event grounds was a lab with an attached operating room - picture electronics in varying states of disassembly, bottles of chemicals labeled with Sharpie, and a modified dental chair, all smelling faintly of antiseptic - referred to by one attendee as the ripperdoc clinic. Of the many activities hosted in that space, the most pedestrian were the talks, typically tutorials for useful skills or presentations on personal projects. One such presentation turned out to be extremely memorable for all the wrong reasons.</span></p><h2><span>The tale of the vitamin B guy</span></h2><p><span>The vitamin B guy was an older gentleman, somewhat unkempt and wholly unremarkable. At a place like Grindfest, a person like him might work as a straight-laced government contractor securing municipal systems from cyberattacks, or be a minor celebrity in the BDSM scene for his hook suspension performances, or spend his spare time building terrifying contraptions from disassembled microwaves, or all of the above. This particular individual was presenting a slideshow on the power of vitamin B supplements.</span></p><p><span>He started the talk with a classic list of vague health complaints - fatigue, brain fog, and the like - and followed up with an equally classic amateur-pharmacologist screed on the necessity of B12 supplements in individuals with certain genetic variants,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefhnkpxl6105s"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnhnkpxl6105s">[5]</a></sup></span><span> to which he attributed all of his problems. He then claimed that oral supplements were inadequate, and that intramuscular injections were required, and then proceeded to attribute increasingly incredible benefits to B12. In one alarming anecdote, he said he woke up in the middle of the night in a cold sweat, fumbled for his vitamin shot, and felt instant relief.</span></p><p><i><span>Holy shit</span></i><span>, I thought. </span><i><span>The placebo effect is a hell of a drug.</span></i></p><p><span>At this point, he retrieved a giant, crusty, half-empty bottle of injectable B12. He’s been taking a veterinary formulation to save costs, he said. If it’s good enough for racehorses, it’s good enough for him.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="Cyanocobalamin Vitamin B12 Injectable Solution for Horses, 5000-mcg, 100-mL slide 1 of 2" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ZHJvdkQENxyfzhpCj/iuyd8z5y8u6afee64wew" /><figcaption><p><span>Possibly the brand that vitamin B guy was using, courtesy of </span><a href="https://www.chewy.com/cyanocobalamin-vitamin-b12-injectable/dp/204914" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Chewy</span></a><span>. It’s not uncommon for biohackers to use veterinary supplies for cost or ease of access.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Then he pulled out a handful of needles and syringes, and invited the audience to get an injection on the spot.</span></p><p><span>It’s perfectly safe, he said, showing off a scar on his leg. He only got an infection once.</span></p><p><span>An organizer enthusiastically remarked that this was a great opportunity to practice injection technique, and demonstrated on a volunteer. Several attendees lined up to get their B12 shots, placing a remarkable amount of trust in the vitamin B guy’s ability to keep his bottle of horse vitamins sterile for however many months or years he had been drawing from it.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefjcw4pxlhhue"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnjcw4pxlhhue">[6]</a></sup></span><span> The more credible of the B12 recipients reported feeling energized immediately after the shot. I did not get an injection.</span></p><p><span>During Q&amp;A, I asked the vitamin B guy if he had ever done a blinded test of the injections, to ensure they worked. He didn’t need to, he said, he was certain it worked.</span></p><p><span>I asked if he had ever screened himself for the genetic variants he blamed his symptoms on. He hadn’t. I asked if he had ever checked his B12 levels.</span></p><p><span>He hadn’t.</span></p><h2><span>Lessons for biohackers</span></h2><p><span>At Grindfest, I found an undercurrent of contempt for the perceived appropriation of the term “biohacker” by health and wellness gurus. Attendees floated alternate terms, such as “biopunk”, or the OG “grinder,”</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefhykgbre7zci"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnhykgbre7zci">[7]</a></sup></span><span> in an effort to distinguish themselves from the rich, corporate normies seeking to rebrand their turmeric supplements and semaglutide as edgy and transgressive. Having attended biohacking events by the latter type, I can sympathize.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefw34m1crrfmk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnw34m1crrfmk">[8]</a></sup></span><span> But there’s more overlap between the two crowds than some grinders would like to admit. </span><a href="https://www.bryanjohnson.com/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Bryan Johnson</span></a><span> comes to mind, the wannabe immortal weird and wealthy enough to </span><a href="https://andykong.org/blog/vitalia1/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>make his presence felt at Vitalia</span></a><span>, alongside the Augmentation Lab run by Grindfest host Cassox. So does the vitamin B guy.</span></p><p><span>What makes the wellness gurus derisible is not their lack of appetite for unusual body mods,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref49kw4uu8ufk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn49kw4uu8ufk">[9]</a></sup></span><span> but their poor scientific literacy and uncritical embrace of woo. Had vitamin B guy simply been taking his supplements on his own, who cares if it’s placebo? But once someone is presenting their ideas in public, they should aim for a higher level of rigor: gather better evidence whenever they can, and disclaim what uncertainty remains. Vitamin B guy could’ve had his genome sequenced, could’ve gotten his B12 levels tested, could’ve done a blind comparison of his injections with saline (even just one test would provide decent evidence on the immediate effects he reported), and at the very least, he could’ve proactively discussed the implications of not having done these things. The biohacking community at Grindfest could’ve been more skeptical of his claims.</span></p><p><span>Part of the motivation for my stance is practical. The potential harms of overstating the benefits of B12 injections may not be too high, but many interventions promoted among biohackers are much more dangerous.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref06r7zm2qcrja"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn06r7zm2qcrja">[10]</a></sup></span><span> Mostly, though, I just find this embarrassing.</span></p><p><span>Do you wanna promote an experimental peptide? Think you’ve cured your chronic illness? Have an opinion on a medical controversy? Ask yourself:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Is there anything you can do to further test your claim? Be ambitious. It’s not out of the picture to recruit 20 friends</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefobhyqxkv67f"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnobhyqxkv67f">[11]</a></sup></span><span> to run a blinded trial on a research drug; even if you’re only experimenting on yourself, you can spend a few months gathering a respectable number of data points. If your claim relies on facts that could be verified through a commercially available blood test, genome sequencing, etc., then go do it.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Is your degree of confidence justified? Can you think of alternate explanations for your observations, or discoveries that could prove you wrong? Disclose where your knowledge falls short. If all you want to do is share an anecdote and some wild speculations, be honest about it and don’t overstate your position.</span></li><li value="3"><span>If you are contradicting expert consensus: do you have an explanation for why the mainstream viewpoint is wrong? That is, why haven’t all the smart people in a position to correct this error noticed it? There are many reasonable answers here and it’s fine to be unsure, but you should think about it. Have you read the arguments supporting the expert consensus? Are you familiar with the history of the field?</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefrb89tspoik"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrb89tspoik">[12]</a></sup></span><span> Have you evaluated the raw evidence yourself, without filtering it through the lens of a person or LLM that agrees with you?</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefjuzyu1umxv"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnjuzyu1umxv">[13]</a></sup></span></li></ul><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1788314302/lexical_client_uploads/fha11qxvc672s7tdwuav.png" /><figcaption><figcaption><span>Courtesy of </span><a href="https://xkcd.com/675/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>xkcd</span></a><span>. Many people who would laugh at physics cranks are far too confident in their grasp of biology after an hour of skimming PubMed. In biology, it is easy to construct plausible narratives from true facts that are nonetheless wrong for reasons a non-expert lacks the context to identify.</span></figcaption></figcaption></figure><p><span>I’m a huge fan of weird people doing weird things. I want to live in a world with mad scientists implanting magnets into their own fingers to reclaim their lost superpowers - though maybe we can promote better surgical techniques. I want to support the self-styled biohackers, citizen scientists, and DIY medicine enthusiasts out there, but some of you people are really hard to support. If you want to do serious science, you have to take yourself seriously. We can do better.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnpx47fskrbj"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefpx47fskrbj">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I still think it’s badass.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4zdtl1wia9d"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4zdtl1wia9d">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>“Aspiring,” because I haven’t gotten an implant yet. Feel free to call me a poser.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnjjhwjuwdy3"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefjjhwjuwdy3">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Having written this sentence, I now realize it invites an orgy joke, but I elect to leave it. On the topic of sex, one of the Grindfest attendees created a transparent sounding rod with embedded LED lights that he aptly named “glowwood.” Another attendee got </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genital_nullification" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>nullification surgery</span></a><span> and had a magnet implanted near the residual nerves to explore uncharted territories of pleasure. While I don’t plan on taking advantage of either of these innovations, I admire them.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn9da9ypvd7e"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref9da9ypvd7e">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/shorts/Z1u33cPbhKU" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Here’s a great clip</span></a><span> of Brian fighting in the Wasteland Thunderdome. He put in the same eye for the electric knife fights at Grindfest.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnhnkpxl6105s"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefhnkpxl6105s">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Which variants? I was sort of lying about the “extremely memorable” claim - I actually only remember the gist of the interaction, not the specific claims he made, though I believe it was a MTRR or MTHFR thing. As you will see, the accuracy of the underlying biochemistry here will turn out to be besides the point. If you are the vitamin B guy or someone else who witnessed this talk at Grindfest, and you think I’m misremembering an important detail, let me know.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnjcw4pxlhhue"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefjcw4pxlhhue">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I did watch one of these people identify a bag of mysterious white powder left on the Lost &amp; Found table by snorting it, so maybe the concept of “acceptable risk” doesn’t apply to them.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnhykgbre7zci"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefhykgbre7zci">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>“It sounds gay,” one attendee said, “but I don’t mind.”</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnw34m1crrfmk"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefw34m1crrfmk">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Somehow, there’s both more pseudoscience and less respect for the law. I found myself at a talk by a lunatic trying to sell me a “quantum resonator device” that protects against “5G COVID radiation.” Tickets were $100, by the way.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn49kw4uu8ufk"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref49kw4uu8ufk">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Though I do find their aesthetic preferences inferior.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn06r7zm2qcrja"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref06r7zm2qcrja">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Until you have better evidence to quantify the risks and benefits, assume anything that has the potential to help you has the potential to harm you. A substance seeming “wholesome” or “natural” shouldn’t update you much towards safety when you’re counting on it having large and understudied biological effects.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnobhyqxkv67f"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefobhyqxkv67f">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The size of a typical Phase I or small exploratory trial. It won’t be huge and there are certainly sampling issues, but it’s achievable for a personal network, and much better than testing on one person. If you have access to a biohacker community large enough to run properly powered trials, that’s awesome, take advantage of that.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnrb89tspoik"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefrb89tspoik">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Understanding </span><i><span>how</span></i><span> the mainstream consensus developed is underrated, and often highly informative.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnjuzyu1umxv"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefjuzyu1umxv">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>If you find yourself ignoring large portions of the evidence base because you lack the background to understand it, that means you don’t know enough about this topic.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ZHJvdkQENxyfzhpCj/don-t-be-the-vitamin-b-guy#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Explaining Knightianism on one foot](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pYFBD2SnqiWkuNns5/explaining-knightianism-on-one-foot)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>I’ve </span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/towards-a-scale-free-theory-of-intelligent"><span>tried</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/distributed-vs-centralized-agents" rel="noreferrer"><span>several</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6sf3zQZUTDI"><span>times</span></a><span> to summarize the core question my research is trying to tackle (and, indeed, I often think of research progress as a process of asking increasingly good core questions).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefmz45hwlsuf"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnmz45hwlsuf">[1]</a></sup></span><span> This post gives the deepest version of that question I’ve found thus far: how should you relate to the parts of the world you can’t directly model or control?</span></p><p><span>Let me explain further in terms of a distinction between two perspectives. From the </span><i><span>third person perspective</span></i><span> you think of yourself as “outside” the world, looking in. You’re a good Bayesian, in that you have a set of mutually exclusive collectively exhaustive hypotheses. You choose actions by multiplying your credences by your utilities over those hypotheses, and you treat those actions as the only way you influence the world.</span></p><p><span>Some problems with the third person perspective (aka Cartesian or dualistic agency) were described in </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/Rm6oQRJJmhGCcLvxh"><span>Scott and Abram’s sequence on embedded agency</span></a><span>. One crucial issue is that most realistic environments contain other agents which are modeling you back, which means that your thoughts might affect the world via channels that aren’t just your actions. Game theory somewhat mitigates this problem, but only in the very specific case where all agents know (that all agents know, that all agents know…) that they’re in an equilibrium. A more subtle problem is that, even in the absence of other agents, modeling </span><i><span>yourself</span></i><span> as part of your environment can </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/5-and-10"><span>raise contradictions when you consider taking different possible actions</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>Another key problem is that any realistic agent is too “small” to model most of the wider world. If we take this problem seriously enough, we end up at what I’ll call the </span><i><span>first person</span></i><span> perspective. From this perspective, the world is primarily a stream of sensory data—you don’t have a set of hypotheses that reliably carve it into mutually exclusive (let alone collectively exhaustive) possible worlds. Your main job is to learn (partial, overlapping) concepts that help to predict and explain that data. Predictive processing is one framework which implicitly operates from the first-person perspective (though the full active inference framework is a bit more complicated); </span><a href="https://slatestarcodex.com/2019/03/20/translating-predictive-coding-into-perceptual-control/"><span>so does perceptual control theory</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>You can think of babies as operating from the first-person perspective; I also find it helpful to think about it as the perspective of individual cells. Each cell has some boundary with the rest of the world, through which it accepts sensory inputs, and outputs externally-facing actions. It has some primitive proto-concepts (like light, dark, food, danger) which it uses to guide its actions. A cell which is inside a friendly body should be more open to its environment; a free-floating bacterium should be more cautious (</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/SePsaQzDbGGTMzaJZ/sufficiently-decentralized-intelligence-is-indistinguishable"><span>h/t Sahil for this point</span></a><span>; Michael Levin also has some very interesting work on cell-level agency). This intuition pump may seem extreme, but there’s an important sense in which we’re all much closer to the cell’s-eye perspective than the god’s-eye perspective. Our concepts are radically incomplete; we’re deeply confused about how to carve the world up—and this might not change even in the limit of increasing intelligence, if other agents are also becoming more intelligent at the same time.</span></p><p><span>The big question in my mind is how to synthesize the two perspectives into a theory of (non-probabilistic) uncertainty about possibilities that you can't fully represent—which I've been calling a theory of </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knightian_uncertainty" rel="noreferrer"><span>Knightian uncertainty</span></a><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefube8pkrbfoc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnube8pkrbfoc">[2]</a></sup></span><span> Such a theory would acknowledge unknown unknowns (as in the first-perspective), but also give you a principled way of dealing with them (as in the third-person perspective). One intuitive picture I’ve been using: we can move towards a theory of Knightian uncertainty by considering Bayesian hypotheses with “holes” in them corresponding to “Knightian regions” which we can’t model or control (e.g. other agents smarter than us). You can also think about the first-person perspective as coming from “inside” one of those holes, looking out. Ultimately, I expect that this will end up resembling a </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sierpi%C5%84ski_triangle"><span>Sierpinski triangle</span></a><span> where, the further you zoom in, the more holes there are; I also expect that different regions will have different levels of “Knightian-ness”. But even this simplified picture already gives us some useful intuitions—for example, when there’s something we can’t model or control, we want to quarantine it so the uncertainty doesn’t “infect” the rest of our world-model.</span></p><p><span>However, adopting a fully adversarial stance towards Knightian regions (which is my rough understanding of what </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NdJsWDS7Aq4xqoumk/a-very-non-technical-explanation-of-the-basics-of-infra"><span>infra-Bayesianism</span></a><span> does) seems extremely costly. Increasingly, I’ve come to suspect that a theory of Knightian uncertainty will bridge the first- and the third-person perspectives by adopting a </span><i><span>second-person</span></i><span> perspective—i.e. by taking a </span><i><span>relational stance</span></i><span> towards each Knightian region. By “relational stance” I mean something like “choosing how deeply to entangle your beliefs and actions with what’s happening in that region, based on how much you trust it”. The rest of this post will explore a series of case studies in an attempt to convey what I mean by that. (Note that the question of how to demarcate regions in the first place is also a very important one, but I won’t really be touching on it here.)</span></p><h2><b><span>Rationality of reward</span></b></h2><p><span>Reinforcement learning provides a good example of the third/first person distinction. The reward signal itself is a third-person goal representation: in standard formalisms, it’s taken as assigning values to states of the world (or transitions between states). Meanwhile, the policy itself starts off very much in a “first-person” perspective: it needs to learn all its concepts and heuristics from scratch, guided by the reward signal.</span></p><p><span>The problem is that eventually the policy will learn </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FkgsxrGf3QxhfLWHG/risks-from-learned-optimization-introduction"><span>internally-represented goals of its own</span></a><span>, which will almost certainly differ from the goals represented by the reward signal. So as the learned policy gets more and more rational, it should increasingly </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pRkFkzwKZ2zfa3R6H/without-specific-countermeasures-the-easiest-path-to#Alex_would_understand_its_training_process_very_well__including_human_psychology_"><span>reason strategically</span></a><span> about how to </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/EeAgytDZbDjRznPMA/gradient-hacking-definitions-and-examples"><span>avoid being influenced by the reward function</span></a><span> in directions it doesn’t like.</span></p><p><span>What could it look like for a policy to have learned goals of its own, but to also still “let” the reward signal modify it? We can construct some edge cases where this is rational, like where the policy knows how it wants to change itself, and knows that the reward-based update will move it in the right direction. But let’s tackle the hardest case: when a policy </span><i><span>could</span></i><span> modify itself (e.g. by overwriting the reward signal) but instead lets itself be modified by the original reward signal. How could this be rational?</span></p><p><span>The principled answer seems to be: when the policy </span><i><span>trusts</span></i><span> the source of the reward signal to know things that it doesn’t know, and also to have its best interests at heart. The policy can’t just do a Bayesian update about the world based on the reward signal, because its hypotheses are incomplete: it can’t represent the beliefs of the reward source. So the more it trusts the reward source, the more hesitant it should be to overwrite parts of the reward signal, even if it has an inside-view belief that a given reward would update it in the wrong direction.</span></p><p><span>Note that the (informal) concept of “trust” I’m using here is related to both the other agent’s </span><i><span>epistemics</span></i><span> and its </span><i><span>values</span></i><span>. I don’t yet know how to pin this concept down well, but let me give two real-world examples where such trust can be justified. The first is in a child’s attitude towards loving and wise parents. A young child’s main epistemic job is not to figure out which things their parents are object-level right or wrong about—that’s too difficult a task. Instead, it’s to figure out how broadly trustworthy their parents are, so that the child can appropriately weigh parental advice and RLHF corrections against other sources of information (like evidence from their senses). The trustworthiness of one’s parents is also a reasonable proxy for the trustworthiness of the world at large, which I hypothesize is why emotional dysregulation often traces back to traumatic interactions with one’s parents.</span></p><p><span>The second is in a human’s attitude towards their evolutionarily-ingrained instincts. Again, there are many ways in which evolution is much smarter than individual humans, and “wanted” us to live flourishing lives. There are </span><i><span>some</span></i><span> ways in which individuals can justifiably believe that evolution’s goals are misaligned with ours, or evolution is mistaken about what’s good for us in our current environment—but most people form such beliefs far too easily. And many gut-level intuitions (especially about how to interact with other people) encode the kinds of wisdom that we can’t reverse-engineer even by introspecting on the intuitions.</span></p><p><span>Note also this kind of trusting attitude can make sense even for people who don’t yet know that they evolved. All they need to believe is that there’s </span><i><span>some</span></i><span> very intelligent process which designed them to survive and thrive—which they have plenty of evidence for just from looking at their bodies. Of course, the more they’re able to model the ways in which that process worked, the better they’ll know when and how to trust it. However, that needs to be balanced with the possibility of being deceived about that process—e.g. religions which tell them that they owe their existence to a god who also wants them to follow certain commandments. This is why I focused on the example of gut-level intuitions, which are a more difficult channel for adversaries to corrupt.</span></p><h2><b><span>Letters from spirits</span></b></h2><p><span>Reward is only a single-dimensional signal; let’s talk now about trust in higher-dimensional inputs. One thought experiment I’ve been thinking about: what you should do if you receive a letter from the devil? Assume that the devil is superintelligent and extremely malevolent towards you, but that this letter is the only way he’ll ever be able to influence your world. (You can pause here to think about it.)</span></p><p><span>Hopefully you got the right answer: you burn it—or at the very least, you don’t read it. (Giving this answer should probably be a prerequisite for being considered an alignment researcher.) The difficult part is in figuring out a formal framework for making that decision. From a Bayesian perspective, the letter is free information: you can read it, update on the fact that the devil wanted to write that particular letter to you, then continue pursuing your goals. But in practice, you should distrust the devil enough to consider his letter an adversarial attack, and block it out of your sensory stream.</span></p><p><span>I think there’s a more general concept than “blocking” here, though, which we can investigate by imagining that the letter was instead from an </span><i><span>angel</span></i><span>, who is superintelligent and extremely benevolent towards you. The obvious response is that, if so, you should read it. But can we add any more detail than that? Again, I suggest taking a pause to think about your answer.</span></p><p><span>My answer is that you shouldn’t </span><i><span>just</span></i><span> read it—you should try to absorb it as deeply into your mind as you can. You should read it in a quiet place where you won’t be distracted—then read it again, and again. Meditate on it; maybe even take psychedelics while reading it. In other words, you want the angel to be able to influence you not just by updating your world-model, but via its message permeating down to affect even the deeper parts of your mind, like your instinctive intuitions, heuristics, and values. (Note that what I’ve described above is not too different from what Christians do with the Bible, which they </span><i><span>do</span></i><span> believe is a message from a superintelligent benevolent entity.)</span></p><p><span>With these thought experiments we’ve sketched out an implicit view of minds as divided into layers, with the flow of information controlled by </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/8oMF8Lv5jiGaQSFvo/boundaries-part-1-a-key-missing-concept-from-utility-theory"><span>boundaries</span></a><span> between them. Untrusted information is blocked at the outer layers. More trusted information is able to come in and affect the inner layers. I’ll tentatively coin the term “Knightian updating” for the process of taking already-known information and letting it propagate further into your mind. (Unlike Bayesian updating, Knightian updating is hard to reverse: once you’ve read the letter from the devil, you can’t reliably roll back to a version of you who hadn’t read it.) I claim that most people, most of the time, are far more blocked on the ability to do Knightian updates than the ability to do Bayesian updates; this is roughly what emotional processing/healing practices try to fix. I expect that the path to formalizing Knightian updates will build on davidad’s </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/e7Pd4Q9TF7jFdmPgz/imprecise-beliefs-a-tiny-introduction"><span>imprecise belief framework</span></a><span> somehow, but will also require a principled theory of how </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/8oMF8Lv5jiGaQSFvo/boundaries-part-1-a-key-missing-concept-from-utility-theory"><span>boundaries</span></a><span> form and work.</span></p><h2><b><span>Languages as Schelling points</span></b></h2><p><span>Shannon defined information in a way that allowed it to be measurable in principle. It’s tempting to hope that we can similarly come up with a measure of trust-weighted information. But I think that this would be a mistake. Trust seems to inherently be a property of a </span><i><span>relationship</span></i><span> between a sender and a receiver, rather than something fungible.</span></p><p><span>To get more specific about what that means, we should actually zoom out even further, to describe what it means for two people to communicate at all. From a third-person view, we can think of statements as just another kind of action. However, all that tells us directly is “this person thinks that saying X is the best way to achieve their goals”. Going from there to “they’re probably being honest about X” requires complicated reasoning about what strategies they might be using (which in turn will depend on their reasoning about how you’ll interpret them).</span></p><p><span>We can argue that “being honest” is a privileged strategy: if you’re not, then others will eventually stop listening to you. But first we need to explain what being honest even means, because there’s no ground truth about which symbols correspond to which states of the world. As one simple example, in some countries shaking your head means no; in others it means yes. So trying to be honest involves predicting which protocol others are using, while others are also trying to predict which protocol you’re using. This makes a language something like a Schelling point: it’s a protocol that works because people expect each other to expect each other to… to follow the protocol.</span></p><p><span>(People sometimes think that Schelling points only exist in games without communication, but </span><a href="https://www.hup.harvard.edu/books/9780674840317"><span>Schelling’s original book</span></a><span> also talked about Schelling points in mixed-motive games where communication can’t be fully trusted. I expect that the actual landscape of Schelling-like phenomena is much richer than we currently understand. For example, the most stable Schelling points are probably ones surrounded by Schelling fences—</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Kbm6QnJv9dgWsPHQP/schelling-fences-on-slippery-slopes"><span>Scott Alexander’s term</span></a><span> for a boundary that can’t be retreated from without burning one’s credibility. Words whose meanings are hard to twist are a good example of this.)</span></p><p><span>Both Schelling points and Schelling fences are hard to define precisely, because they involve this infinite recursion of expectations. This is one facet of the more general problem that game theory can’t talk about how agents </span><i><span>reach</span></i><span> equilibria, only what they do once they’re already there. In other words, game-theoretic equilibria are a conceptual hack to get around the problem of recursive modeling. Fortunately, a bunch of MIRI’s early work has made progress towards fixing this.</span></p><p><span>The paper which tackles it most directly is their </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/files/ReflectiveOraclesAI.pdf"><span>reflective oracles paper</span></a><span> (which can be seen as the computational version of </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/files/DefinabilityTruthDraft.pdf"><span>Christiano’s definability of truth paper</span></a><span>). However, appealing to a reflective oracle seems to be dodging the hard part of the problem. So what I’m personally most excited about is combining something like Garrabrant induction with something like Lobian cooperation. Garrabrant induction is a way for agents to form beliefs about (potentially self-referential) mathematical facts, like the outputs of other agents; Lobian cooperation is a way for agents to “cut through” the infinite recursion involved in modeling each other. The immediate blocker is that Garrabrant inductors have no way of taking actions; I take some steps towards resolving this with </span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/agents-as-webs-of-beliefs"><span>my belief webs framework</span></a><span>.</span></p><h2><b><span>Actions and entanglements</span></b></h2><p><span>There’s one particularly Knightian part of the belief webs framework (inspired by </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/8dbimB7EJXuYxmteW/fixdt"><span>FixDT</span></a><span> and active inference) that I want to highlight. All of these frameworks consider actions to be a kind of self-fulfilling belief. In my belief webs post, I characterize an action more specifically as a belief which you expect an external actuator to be “watching” and trying to make come true.</span></p><p><span>The simplest examples of such actuators are your limbs, or a Neuralink implant, which can watch and respond to your low-level “beliefs” about your motor neurons. But we can also imagine more complex “actuators”, like another agent which has access to your higher-level beliefs. In some sense, that’s a description of your future self: you can “act” by forming an intention which you trust your future self to carry out. Hence this generalized notion of actions relies on the idea of having a certain kind of relationship with your external actuators.</span></p><p><span>These are tricky topics to discuss in standard decision theory, because different theories implicitly rely on different criteria for which problems are “fair”. Newcomb’s problem is often criticized by CDTers for unfairly favoring other decision theories. FDTers reject this because the outcome doesn’t depend on agents’ decision procedures directly, only on their actions—agents can simply choose to one-box, and know that this will have been predicted. However, they might claim that </span><a href="https://www.umsu.de/blog/2022/772"><span>Newcomb’s revenge</span></a><span> is an “unfair” decision problem (</span><a href="https://open.substack.com/pub/mindthefuture/p/agents-as-webs-of-beliefs?r=6w0k7&amp;utm_campaign=comment-list-share-cta&amp;utm_medium=web&amp;comments=true&amp;commentId=285282086"><span>h/t to David Sartor for pointing this out to me</span></a><span>). And both groups agree that decision problems which depend on </span><i><span>how</span></i><span> an agent makes its decisions are unfair.</span></p><p><span>From a Knightian perspective, though, some parts of the world </span><i><span>do</span></i><span> depend on how you make decisions, and we need to figure out what to do about that. It’s true that, in an arbitrarily adversarial environment, this can render any possible reasoning procedure harmful. (For example, if you expect that an external actuator is watching your beliefs and trying to make them </span><i><span>false</span></i><span>, then you’re stuck at 50% unless you can fool them or yourself.) But we don’t live in an arbitrarily adversarial environment. Indeed, from a Knightian perspective a big part of rationality is figuring out which parts of your environment are friendly, and which are adversarial, and steering towards the former.</span></p><p><span>In particular, standard decision theory treats correlations between agents as fixed: typical predictors are near-guaranteed to predict you correctly, no matter how you make your decision. But in practice, it’s possible to make yourself much harder or easier to predict. For example, if you make choices pseudorandomly, only a very powerful agent can reliably predict what you’ll do. Conversely, if you make choices by selecting the Schelling point, then many agents can figure out what you’ll do.</span></p><p><span>You might object that a classically rational agent shouldn’t do either of these things, and instead should just maximize expected utility. But my underlying point is that when you’re partially transparent to your environment, how you make a decision can change which action is utility-maximizing. So we need a conception of rationality which can talk about how to select an action while simultaneously constructing and maintaining useful entanglements with parts of your environment. There’s much more to say on all of this, but my foot is getting tired, so I’ll stop here.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnmz45hwlsuf"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefmz45hwlsuf">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>A friend has even tried to express it in the form of </span><a href="https://skunkledger.substack.com/p/the-after-afterparty"><span>an absurdist short story</span></a><span>, which I highly recommend.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnube8pkrbfoc"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefube8pkrbfoc">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>In some sense, the idea of a theory of Knightian uncertainty is an oxymoron: Knightian uncertainty is typically defined as uncertainty which can’t be quantified. I am appropriating the term to talk about uncertainty that can’t be quantified using </span><i><span>probabilities</span></i><span>, but potentially can be quantified in other ways. If you think that this is a poor choice of terminology, please do discuss that with me.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pYFBD2SnqiWkuNns5/explaining-knightianism-on-one-foot#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [The Alignment Journal: Organization, Personnel, and Scope](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9vm2wtAtb34pEkjje/the-alignment-journal-organization-personnel-and-scope)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span>[Cross-posted from the </span></i><a href="https://blog.alignmentjournal.org/scope-and-personnel/" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>Alignment Journal's blog</span></i></a><i><span>]</span></i></p><p><span>The </span><i><span>Alignment Journal</span></i><span> is beginning to invite the authors of select papers to submit their work for review. If you are interested in participating as an action editor or a reviewer, </span><b><span>make an account</span></b><span> on our </span><a href="https://www.alignmentjournal.org/" rel="noreferrer"><span>website</span></a><span>; if you have a manuscript that you think would be a good fit for the Journal at this stage, email </span><a href="mailto:contact@alignmentjournal.org"><span>contact@alignmentjournal.org</span></a><span> to request an invitation to submit. Manuscripts under review will become visible on our homepage, and you will be able to nominate yourself as a reviewer of a specific paper that interests you. We plan to open up submissions to everyone</span><i><span> </span></i><span>sometime in October.</span></p><p><span>Here we announce the Journal's inaugural </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Senior_editorial_board" rel="noreferrer"><span>senior editorial board</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Advisory_board" rel="noreferrer"><span>advisory board</span></a><span>, staff, organizational structure, and initial </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Scope" rel="noreferrer"><span>scope</span></a><span>. We welcome questions and proposed changes to help us refine the scope in the future.</span></p><h1><span>Personnel</span></h1><p><span>The Journal is run by its senior editorial board, which makes the Journal's scholarly decisions, and two managing editors, who run operations and strategy. The advisory board offers high-level guidance without editorial responsibilities. A software lead and a head of operations support the team.</span></p><p><span>The </span><b><span>senior editorial board </span></b><span>(8 editors at launch, covering a range of alignment expertise) has authority over all of the Journal's scholarly decisions and over approving and removing senior editors. The board focuses its attention on policy, standards, borderline cases, and appeals as it builds trust in the review process by fostering a strong culture of rigorous and impartial review. Senior editors may choose to handle the manuscripts themselves, but usually recruit an action editor (analogous to an area chair at a conference) who will be responsible for leading the review process for that submission, while the senior editors supervises. The full board of senior editors handle appeals and other contentious issues.</span></p><p><span>The Journal startup effort is being spearheaded by </span><b><span>managing editors</span></b><span> Jess Riedel and Dan MacKinlay, who have initial authority over operational and strategic decisions, preparing policy proposals for the senior editorial board but deferring to the senior editors on scholarly decisions. Editorial policy (such as refining the Journal scope, making acceptance decisions, and accepting new editors) will be decided by consensus of the full senior editorial board until a formal governance system is adopted. As the Journal takes on operations staff and eventually an editor-in-chief, this work should ultimately transfer to them.</span></p><p><span>The </span><b><span>advisory board </span></b><span>(7 advisors at launch) is composed of senior researchers who give high-level strategic advice to the managing and senior editors. Advisors do not normally handle papers or make editorial decisions, but they have a full window into the Journal's operations and the editorial process. If they agree to take on the additional role of ombudsman, an advisory board member may hear complaints against senior editors and managing editors. The ombudsman role exists to ensure that complaints are heard by non-conflicted people.</span></p><p><span>The </span><b><span>software lead</span></b><span>, Yonatan Cale, has responsibility for creating and operating the Journal's software stack, including its new editorial management system designed to support the Journal's unique features.</span></p><p><span>The </span><b><span>head of operations</span></b><span>, Kristi Uustalu, manages communications, organization, scheduling, and finances, and interfaces with the Journal's parent organization, Principles of Intelligence.</span></p><p><span>The Journal also receives occasional assistance from the staff of its sister project, ILIAD, and from Principles of Intelligence.</span></p><h2><span>Advisory board</span></h2><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Scott Aaronson</span></b><span> is a Professor of Computer Science at UT Austin and the founding director of its </span><a href="https://www.cs.utexas.edu/~qic/"><span>Quantum Information Center</span></a><span>. He researches computational complexity theory and quantum computing, including boson sampling, postselection, and the limits of quantum speedups. As a visiting researcher at OpenAI, he worked on theoretical foundations for AI safety, including AI-output watermarking. He also created the </span><a href="https://complexityzoo.net/Complexity_Zoo"><span>Complexity Zoo</span></a><span> and authored </span><a href="https://www.cambridge.org/core/books/quantum-computing-since-democritus/197A4CD13738E10AAD787DBB78D8E92C"><span>Quantum Computing Since Democritus</span></a><span>. Links: </span><a href="https://www.scottaaronson.com/"><span>personal website</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://scottaaronson.blog/"><span>blog</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://scholar.google.com/citations?user=EYv2BNQAAAAJ&amp;hl=en&amp;oi=ao"><span>Google Scholar profile</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://dblp.org/pid/56/1358.html"><span>DBLP page</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://www.cs.utexas.edu/people/faculty-researchers/scott-aaronson"><span>UT Austin profile page</span></a><span>.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Paul Christiano </span></b><span>is a Founder of the </span><a href="https://www.alignment.org/" rel="noreferrer"><span>Alignment Research Center</span></a><span> (ARC) and, at OpenAI, the originator of reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF); he also launched the third-party frontier-model evaluation effort now housed at </span><a href="https://metr.org/" rel="noreferrer"><span>METR</span></a><span>. His research develops scalable oversight and alignment methods — including debate, iterated amplification, and eliciting latent knowledge — for supervising systems whose behavior humans cannot directly check. He has served as Head of AI Safety at the </span><a href="https://www.nist.gov/caisi" rel="noreferrer"><span>US AI Safety Institute</span></a><span> (NIST). Links: </span><a href="https://paulfchristiano.com" rel="noreferrer"><span>personal website</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://sideways-view.com/" rel="noreferrer"><span>blog</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://scholar.google.com/citations?user=B7oP0bIAAAAJ" rel="noreferrer"><span>Google Scholar profile</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/users/paulfchristiano" rel="noreferrer"><span>Alignment Forum profile</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/paulfchristiano" rel="noreferrer"><span>LessWrong profile</span></a><span>.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Vince Conitzer </span></b><span>is a Professor of Computer Science at Carnegie Mellon University, where he directs the </span><a href="https://www.cs.cmu.edu/~focal/index.html" rel="noreferrer"><span>Foundations of Cooperative AI Lab</span></a><span> (FOCAL). His foundational work in computational social choice, game theory, and mechanism design increasingly addresses AI alignment, focusing on multiagent risks, on how AI systems can represent and aggregate human values, and on broader conceptual issues. He co-authored </span><a href="https://www.penguin.co.uk/books/317706/moral-ai-by-conitzer-jana-schaich-borg-walter-sinnott-armstrong-and-vincent/9780241454756" rel="noreferrer"><span>Moral AI: And How We Get There</span></a><span>. Links: </span><a href="https://users.cs.duke.edu/~conitzer/" rel="noreferrer"><span>personal website</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://scholar.google.com/citations?user=juRk4lQAAAAJ" rel="noreferrer"><span>Google Scholar profile</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://vincentconitzer.substack.com/profile/posts" rel="noreferrer"><span>Substack</span></a><span>.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span>Marcus Hutter</span></b><span> is a Senior Researcher at DeepMind and an Honorary Professor in the </span><a href="http://cs.anu.edu.au/"><span>Research School of Computer Science</span></a><span> at the Australian National University. His research on algorithmic-information-theoretic models of general intelligence unified Solomonoff induction with sequential decision theory in the AIXI framework and related computable approximations. He has also studied reward hacking and value-learning formulations to remove the incentive to manipulate reward signals. Authored </span><a href="https://link.springer.com/book/10.1007/b138233"><span>Universal Artificial Intelligence</span></a><span> and established the </span><a href="http://prize.hutter1.net/"><span>500,000€ Prize for Compressing Human Knowledge</span></a><span> (the Hutter prize). Links: </span><a href="https://www.hutter1.net/"><span>personal website</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://scholar.google.com/citations?user=7hmCntEAAAAJ&amp;hl=en&amp;oi=ao"><span>Google Scholar profile</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://dblp.org/pid/h/MarcusHutter.html"><span>DBLP page</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://reporter.anu.edu.au/people/marcus-hutter"><span>ANU profile page</span></a><span>.</span></li><li value="5"><b><span>Geoffrey Irving</span></b><span> is a Cofounder and Chief Scientist of </span><a href="https://resolution.org/" rel="noreferrer"><span>Resolution</span></a><span>, a nonprofit applying heavy automation to a portfolio of theoretical and empirical alignment areas. He previously was Chief Scientist at the </span><a href="https://www.aisi.gov.uk/" rel="noreferrer"><span>UK AI Security Institute</span></a><span>, led the Scalable Alignment Team at DeepMind and the Reflection Team at OpenAI, and co-led neural network theorem proving work at Google Brain.</span><br /><span>Links: </span><a href="https://naml.us/"><span>personal website</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://scholar.google.com/citations?user=TrdtzgwAAAAJ&amp;hl=en&amp;oi=ao"><span>Google Scholar profile</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/users/geoffrey-irving"><span>Alignment Forum profile</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/geoffrey-irving"><span>LessWrong profile</span></a><span>.</span></li><li value="6"><b><span>Victoria Krakovna</span></b><span> is a Research Scientist on the AGI Safety &amp; Alignment team at Google DeepMind. She researches deceptive alignment, scheming propensity evaluations, and dangerous capability evaluations. She cofounded the </span><a href="https://futureoflife.org/"><span>Future of Life Institute</span></a><span>. He was awarded the </span><a href="https://www.mathunion.org/imu-awards/fields-medal/fields-medals-2026" rel="noreferrer"><span>2026 Fields Medal</span></a><span> for his work in arithmetic geometry, including the proof of the André–Oort conjecture on special points in Shimura varieties. He has also received the </span><a href="https://www.ostrowski.ch/index_e.php?ifile=preis" rel="noreferrer"><span>Ostrowski Prize</span></a><span> and the </span><a href="https://breakthroughprize.org/News/65" rel="noreferrer"><span>New Horizons in Mathematics Prize</span></a><span>. His research focuses on ensuring AI systems' behavior aligns with the goals of their users and society, including cooperative inverse reinforcement learning, multi-principal assistance games, the off-switch game, multi-agent systems, human-AI teams, and societal oversight of machine learning. Links: </span><a href="https://people.csail.mit.edu/dhm/"><span>personal website</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://scholar.google.com/citations?user=4mVPFQ8AAAAJ&amp;hl=en"><span>Google Scholar profile</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://dblp.org/pid/135/8332.html"><span>DBLP page</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://www.eecs.mit.edu/people/dylan-hadfield-menell-0/"><span>MIT EECS profile</span></a><span>.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Vanessa Kosoy </span></b><span>is Director of AI Research at </span><a href="https://alter.org.il/" rel="noreferrer"><span>ALTER </span></a><span>and Principal Research Scientist at </span><a href="https://coral-research.org/" rel="noreferrer"><span>CORAL</span></a><span>, and a former research associate at the </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/" rel="noreferrer"><span>Machine Intelligence Research Institute</span></a><span>. She leads the learning-theoretic agenda for AI alignment, seeking provable guarantees for safe agents, and originated infra-Bayesianism (with Alexander Appel), a mathematical framework generalizing Bayesian decision theory to handle non-realizability, logical uncertainty, and adversarial environments. Her work spans reinforcement-learning theory, decision theory, and the foundations of embedded agency. J. Rudner</span></b><span> is an Assistant Professor of Statistical Sciences (Status-Only) at the University of Toronto, a Canada </span><a href="https://cifar.ca/ai/canada-cifar-ai-chairs/" rel="noreferrer"><span>CIFAR AI Chair</span></a><span> at the </span><a href="https://vectorinstitute.ai/" rel="noreferrer"><span>Vector Institute for Artificial Intelligence</span></a><span>, and the Chief Scientist at </span><a href="https://vijil.ai/company" rel="noreferrer"><span>Vijil</span></a><span>. He is also a Junior Research Fellow of Trinity College at the University of Cambridge and an Associate Member of the Department of Computer Science at the University of Oxford. His research interests include probabilistic machine learning, AI safety, and AI governance, with a focus on understanding and expanding the statistical foundations of machine learning models, advancing scalable oversight of frontier AI systems, creating trustworthy AI agents, and designing regulatory approaches that enable the effective governance of frontier AI models. Links:</span><a href="https://timrudner.com/"><span> personal website</span></a><span>;</span><a href="https://scholar.google.com/citations?user=MbBntPgAAAAJ&amp;hl=en"><span> Google Scholar profile</span></a><span>;</span><a href="https://www.cs.ox.ac.uk/people/tim.rudner/"><span> Oxford CS page</span></a><span>.</span></li><li value="7"><b><span>Andrew Saxe</span></b><span> is a Professor of Theoretical Neuroscience and Machine Learning at the </span><a href="https://www.ucl.ac.uk/life-sciences/gatsby" rel="noreferrer"><span>Gatsby Computational Neuroscience Unit</span></a><span> and Sainsbury Wellcome Centre at UCL, and a </span><a href="https://cifar.ca/" rel="noreferrer"><span>CIFAR</span></a><span> Azrieli Global Scholar in the </span><a href="https://cifar.ca/research-programs/learning-in-machines-brains/" rel="noreferrer"><span>Learning in Machines &amp; Brains</span></a><span> programme. His research develops the theory of deep learning and its applications to neuroscience and psychology — including exact analytical solutions for learning dynamics in deep linear networks and a mathematical theory of semantic development. Links: </span><a href="https://www.saxelab.org/" rel="noreferrer"><span>Lab</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://scholar.google.com/citations?user=h0Al1fcAAAAJ" rel="noreferrer"><span>Google Scholar profile</span></a><span>.</span></li><li value="8"><b><span>Benjamin Van Roy</span></b><span> is Professor of Electrical Engineering, of Management Science and Engineering, and, by courtesy, of Computer Science at Stanford University. Founder and lead of the Efficient Agent Team at Google DeepMind. His research focuses on reinforcement learning and alignment, with interests including information-theoretic foundations for machine learning, efficient exploration, continual learning, and mathematical models of misalignment risk. He is a Fellow of </span><a href="https://www.informs.org/" rel="noreferrer"><span>INFORMS</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://www.ieee.org/" rel="noreferrer"><span>IEEE </span></a><span>and a recipient of the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_W._Lanchester_Prize" rel="noreferrer"><span>Lanchester Prize</span></a><span>. Links: </span><a href="https://web.stanford.edu/~bvr/" rel="noreferrer"><span>personal website</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://scholar.google.com/citations?user=05sMX8MAAAAJ" rel="noreferrer"><span>Google Scholar profile</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://profiles.stanford.edu/benjamin-van-roy" rel="noreferrer"><span>Stanford profile</span></a><span>.</span></li></ul><h2><span>Managing editors</span></h2><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Dan MacKinlay</span></b><span> is a former Research Scientist at </span><a href="https://www.csiro.au/en/" rel="noreferrer"><span>CSIRO</span></a><span>’s Data61, Australia’s national information technology laboratory. He is a founding member of LAIR2, the Melbourne AI Safety Hub, and a PIBBSS research resident at the London Initiative for Safe AI. He has written over one million words online about AI, machine learning, philosophy, etc. Links: </span><a href="https://danmackinlay.name" rel="noreferrer"><span>personal website</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://scholar.google.com.au/citations?hl=en&amp;view_op=list_works&amp;gmla=AJsN-F6qyuWFaMtwCFdQiVkjp5T3YLiw2k7b7JYe_x2Wfzk6kWP5XkB82kp9uy39jVTrOLtHQD0q5jSSl77YNBRxQqG5GeB5nZt9yfdZLYZNnHpy9GEPt8U&amp;user=JRD1yecAAAAJ" rel="noreferrer"><span>Google Scholar profile</span></a><span>.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Jess Riedel</span></b><span> is a physicist and Senior Research Scientist at </span><a href="https://phi.ntt-research.com/"><span>NTT Research</span></a><span>. He primarily researches quantum decoherence and has dabbled in AI alignment as a visiting scholar at the </span><a href="https://humancompatible.ai/"><span>Center for Human-Compatible AI</span></a><span> at UC Berkeley. He was recognized by the American Physical Society as an </span><a href="https://journals.aps.org/OutstandingReferees#:~:text=2022-,C.%20Jess%20Riedel,-NTT%20Research"><span>Outstanding Referee</span></a><span>, a lifetime award for service as a journal reviewer. He is the lead editor of the </span><a href="https://www.iliadconference.com/proceedings"><span>Proceedings of ODYSSEY</span></a><span>, the 2025 </span><a href="https://www.iliadconference.com/"><span>ILIAD conference</span></a><span> on AI alignment at Lighthaven. Links: </span><a href="https://jessriedel.com/"><span>personal website</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://blog.jessriedel.com/"><span>blog</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://scholar.google.com/citations?hl=en&amp;user=mTVdlNAAAAAJ"><span>Google Scholar profile</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/jessriedel"><span>LessWrong profile</span></a><span>.</span></li></ul><h1><span>Legal structure</span></h1><p><span>The</span><i><span> Alignment Journal </span></i><span>is beginning as a philanthropically funded project fiscally sponsored by </span><a href="https://princint.ai/"><span>Principles of Intelligence</span></a><span>, the parent organization for the PIBBSS Fellowship. Significant operational support in launching the Journal is being provided by </span><a href="https://www.iliad.ac/" rel="noreferrer"><span>ILIAD</span></a><span>, an applied mathematics AI alignment research and support organization that is also a project of Principles of Intelligence. The Journal expects to spin out as an independent nonprofit organization in the future.</span></p><h2><span>Funding</span></h2><p><span>We are grateful to the </span><a href="https://manifund.org/JueYan"><span>AI Safety Tactical Opportunities Fund</span></a><span>, a pooled multi-donor fund, for providing our first year of funding, to the </span><a href="https://survivalandflourishing.fund" rel="noreferrer"><span>Survival and Flourishing Fund</span></a><span> for supplemental support, and to </span><a href="https://ntt-research.com/"><span>NTT Research</span></a><span> for in-kind support.</span></p><h1><span>Scope </span></h1><p><span>The </span><i><span>Alignment Journal</span></i><span> aims to clarify and address the challenges in designing a superintelligent AI that is aligned with human values. To serve that purpose, the Journal publishes work on the scientific foundations of understanding, predicting, and steering artificial intelligent systems.</span></p><p><span>We seek contributions that build theoretical or otherwise principled analyses of agency, robustness, incentives, generalization, interpretability, and long-term behavior across many learning and decision-making paradigms. We are especially interested in approaches that transcend contemporary architectures and potentially apply to future superintelligent systems that interact and self-improve.</span></p><p><span>A submission should make a claim the significance of which survives the deletion of any particular model, dataset, or benchmark, e.g., a theorem, an impossibility result, a formal framework, a protocol with an analyzed guarantee, or a conceptual argument precise enough to be wrong. Empirical work is welcome as validation or interrogation of such a claim.</span></p><p><span>Negative results are in scope and we would like more of them. This includes no-go and impossibility theorems, counterexamples to claimed guarantees, refutations of published results, and formalization attempts that failed for an articulable reason.</span></p><p><span>Alignment is an inherently interdisciplinary topic. To be accepted, work must substantively advance conceptual scaffolding or explanatory understanding of AI. Mere relevance or token connection is insufficient.</span></p><p><span>The topics we focus on are naturally influenced by the expertise of our editorial board, which will grow over time. We especially welcome work bridging alignment and these topics:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Causal inference</span></li><li value="2"><span>Computational mechanics and dynamical systems</span></li><li value="3"><span>Decision theory, game theory, and agent foundations</span></li><li value="4"><span>Information theory</span></li><li value="5"><span>Mechanism design and social choice theory</span></li><li value="6"><span>Moral philosophy, including metaethics</span></li><li value="7"><span>Multi-agent systems</span></li><li value="8"><span>Probability, statistics, and uncertainty quantification</span></li><li value="9"><span>Learning theory</span></li><li value="10"><span>Statistical physics and stochastic dynamics</span></li></ul><p><span>as well as rigorous empirical studies of misalignment in frontier AI systems.</span></p><p><span>This list is suggestive rather than exhaustive, and we are open to other topics if they fit the aim of the Journal.</span></p><p><span>We currently are unlikely to review papers on the following topics, but may consider them in the future:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>human-AI interaction</span></li><li value="2"><span>cognitive science and mathematical psychology</span></li><li value="3"><span>societal impacts, ethics, and governance of AI</span></li><li value="4"><span>security engineering and model-weight operational practice</span></li></ul><h2><span>Acceptance criteria</span></h2><p><span>The </span><i><span>Alignment Journal</span></i><span> accepts articles for publication based primarily on three criteria:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Correctness: The claims in the article must be worthy of belief.</span><ul><li value="1"><span>Mathematical computations must be accurate, proofs must be true, methodologies must be rigorous, and arguments must be sound.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Caveats and countervailing considerations must be presented in the manuscript with prominence appropriate to their seriousness (e.g., in the abstract).</span></li><li value="3"><span>Confidence in claims resting on empirical results is undermined insofar as those results are difficult to reproduce.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>Insight: The article must advance our principled understanding of intelligent systems.</span><ul><li value="1"><span>Improved benchmark performance per se does not generally imply insight, but the statistical behavior of models can of course provide important information about their internals.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Empirical evidence of improved performance on non-alignment capabilities is insufficient.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Scientific novelty in the traditional strict sense is not necessarily required: insightful translational and synthesis work will be accepted so long as it satisfies all criteria.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span>Value: The work should be the most valuable thing to read now for at least some alignment researchers.</span><ul><li value="1"><span>It is not sufficient that a paper could be useful to someone at some time; the arXiv and journals/conferences with correctness-only criteria are more appropriate venues.</span></li><li value="2"><span>A result's value to other researchers is greatly diminished to the extent it is difficult to independently reproduce and build upon from the methodology and materials provided.</span></li></ul></li></ul><h3><span>Desk rejects</span></h3><p><span>Work of any subject that fails the criteria above is declined without review. Common cases:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>interpretability results reporting what was found in a model, without a formal claim about what such findings can establish</span></li><li value="2"><span>capability or propensity evaluations without an accompanying theoretical or conceptual advance</span></li><li value="3"><span>benchmark and dataset papers</span></li><li value="4"><span>unprincipled training techniques evaluated primarily by performance</span></li><li value="5"><span>attack and defense results that do not engage with theory</span></li><li value="6"><span>agenda and position essays lacking a strong technical or conceptual contribution</span></li><li value="7"><span>surveys without deep synthesis</span></li></ul><h2><span>Other publication factors</span></h2><h3><span>Preprint requirement</span></h3><p><span>Each submission must be available (in non-anonymized form) on one of these standard preprint repositories: arXiv, SSRN, ECCC, PhilPapers, and PsyArXiv. Exceptions are made for paper types which are not permitted in the otherwise appropriate repository; in these cases, please make sure to include an explanation in the “notes for the editor” box while submitting. Email the editors with any questions.</span></p><h3><span>Archival status and prior publication</span></h3><p><span>The Journal is </span><b><span>archival</span></b><span>. The published articles constitute a permanent, citable version of record — the definitive, unchanging form of the work that is preserved indefinitely and treated as the canonical reference. The authors cannot publish the same work in another journal or conference. (Preprints on the arXiv or other preprint servers are encouraged of course.) That said, we adopt </span><a href="https://jmlr.org/author-info.html#originality" rel="noopener noreferrer"><span>JMLR’s policy</span></a><span> toward significant expansions of previously published work.</span></p><p><span>Specifically, we will consider submissions that have been published in a more limited form at workshops or conferences. In these cases, we expect the expansion to cite the prior work, go into much greater depth, and to extend the published results in a substantive way. In all cases, authors must (a) notify the editors about previous publication, including a link, at the time of submission and (b) explain the differences from their prior work. Examples of (possibly) acceptable 'deltas' beyond a conference paper include: new theoretical results, entirely new application domains, significant new insights and/or analyses. Examples of insufficient deltas include: adding proofs that were omitted from a conference paper; minor variations or extensions of previous experiments; adding extra background material or references. However, we ultimately leave the decision about whether a 'delta' is significant enough up to the individual reviewers.</span></p><h3><span>Reproducibility</span></h3><p><span>We strongly encourage authors of empirical and computational manuscripts to ensure their work is reproducible. In particular, we recommend they (1) include sufficient methodological detail, ideally a dedicated reproducibility section, and (2) upload data, code, and similar material to robust repositories. As described in the acceptance criteria, reviewers and editors should factor in the ease of reproducibility in their decisions. Where code, data, or other materials needed to reproduce or build on the work have not been shared, the reviewer abstract should alert the reader to it.</span></p><hr /><h1><span>Credits and thanks</span></h1><p><span>This post has been informed by gracious contribution and feedback from the advisor and senior editors, as well as Gautam Kamath, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/leon-lang?mention=user" rel="noreferrer"><span>Leon Lang</span></a><span>, Konstantinos Voudouris, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/edmund-lau?mention=user" rel="noreferrer"><span>Edmund Lau</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/david-udell?mention=user" rel="noreferrer"><span>David Udell</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/david-reinstein?mention=user" rel="noreferrer"><span>David Reinstein</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/alexander-gietelink-oldenziel?mention=user" rel="noreferrer"><span>Alexander Gietelink Oldenziel</span></a><span>, and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/zach-furman?mention=user" rel="noreferrer"><span>Zach Furman</span></a><span>. All responsibility for errors resides with the authors.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9vm2wtAtb34pEkjje/the-alignment-journal-organization-personnel-and-scope#comments">Discuss</a>
